# Understand the decisions

This record explains material choices and their consequences. Return to [project state](project-state.md) for the index. [Architecture](architecture.md) describes the current shape, [open questions](open-questions.md) records missing facts, and [to dos](todos.md) records follow-up work. Retain superseded entries and name their replacements.

## D001. Establish research evidence before autonomy

Accepted in the [master plan](master-plan.md), merged in [PR 1](https://github.com/sunpar/quant-research/pull/1) on 21 October 2026. First reproduce a researcher-owned experiment through actual frameworks. Add durable execution, source context, and bounded autonomy in later phases.

This sequence makes failures attributable and prevents agent coordination from substituting for numerical verification. A valid null or inconclusive result is acceptable. Synthetic fixtures do not establish real integration acceptance.

## D002. Keep agent tooling and preferences project-local

Accepted during project setup. Vendor the pinned [pstack source](../.agents/pstack-source.json), use the [project model map](../.agents/pstack-models.md), and keep project Superpowers disablement in place. The configured budget is unlimited and the role choices were accepted by the human owner.

Separate model IDs from reasoning effort when dispatching agents. Preserve the two-model, single-provider configuration rather than inventing provider diversity. [AGENTS.md](../AGENTS.md) defines the workflow entry point and the model-file override.

## D003. Deliver three local increments before the real adapter

Selected on 21 October 2026 in [the initial PR plan](initial-prs.md). Build exact-snapshot verification, portable experiment packages, then causal development-fixture replay. Each increment must have an executable consumer and observable behavior checks.

The actual frameworks, dataset, baseline, and owners are missing. Local records and fixtures can advance without guessing those interfaces. The next real adapter remains dependent on [Q001 through Q004](open-questions.md).

## D004. Maintain the project record with every PR

Required by the human owner on 21 October 2026 and narrowed on 22 October 2026. Keep architecture, decisions, open questions, and tasks navigable from [project state](project-state.md). A PR updates the pages whose facts it changes; it does not log documentation reviews or test-run timings. Link to GitHub for changing PR and check state.

## D005. Verify disposable snapshots with prepared dependencies

Selected for implementation on 21 October 2026 after two architecture candidates and an independent cross-judge. [The initial PR plan](initial-prs.md#selected-architecture) records the comparison. Use one repository runner, raw Git-object exports, and dependency-only environment generations. Copy the supplied index before capturing its tree.

Actual probes showed that `write-tree` changed index bytes and archive export attributes omitted a tracked file. The push hook checks every outgoing branch tip to avoid uncertain branch/PR eligibility lookups. Since 22 October 2026 that check is the smoke suite, the PR wrapper adds no full run, and the full suite runs once on the final commit before merge. PR metadata and lookup failures stop before pushing. Checks use matching prepared dependencies and never repair their executing environment.

The first dependency profile permits registry sources and the root project's virtual entry. Direct archive, Git, local, editable, workspace, mixed, and unknown source kinds are rejected. Temporary relative wheel paths did not retain a matching uv source identity across exports. The behavioral fixtures instead use real wheels from a local file-based package index and exercise dependency changes across separate exports. The original dependency checks read installed metadata and validated provided RECORD hashes and sizes, then ran uv's offline locked check. That profile could not detect a coordinated rewrite of installed files and their RECORD. [D008](#d008-seal-fresh-generations-and-reconcile-publication) strengthens this documented limit with evidence captured during fresh preparation.

A bounded loopback probe with uv 0.10.9 produced a direct HTTP wheel's source under `url`, while the tested file URL was rejected by uv. The native review's claim that this version installed a file archive through an `archive` lock key was not reproduced. The explicit registry profile replaces permissive handling of other source shapes without relying on that claim.

Accept repeated exports and conservative environment cache misses. These costs keep the operation boundary explicit. The first gate covers POSIX branch pushes; tag pushes remain outside its scope. Package build verification is included with the first application package in PR 2. See [architecture](architecture.md).

Preparation always creates another permanent-path generation. This prevents an active verifier from observing an environment rewritten by another command, at the cost of retained disk space. There is no automatic garbage collection. Manual removal of unreferenced generations requires all commands, Git hooks, and preparation processes across every linked worktree sharing the store to be stopped. Keep every pointer-referenced generation. A generation absent from a pointer may still be in use until those processes finish.

The initial implementation launched the working runner before capture and allowed exported links to traverse outside the tree. Its first native Astra review identified both gaps. [D007](#d007-install-the-git-boundary-and-execute-the-selected-snapshots-policy) replaces that launch boundary; the original checkpoint does not establish the corrected behavior.

## D006. Keep development packages self-contained

Selected for the portable-package increment on 21 October 2026. Use complete directory revisions with a versioned canonical manifest and all referenced artifact bytes. This gives the first save/inspect consumer a portable record without a journal projector, artifact service, or mutable registry.

The implementation retains stable lineage identity, distinct trial/run/attempt identities, complete supplied attempts and exposures, a disclosure coverage value, and unavailable confirmation. Generic package records preserve outcomes and named artifacts; the replay module owns numerical meaning. A package selects one trial, or none since [D060](#d060-let-a-package-record-no-selected-trial), while carrying the supplied trial history and a shared exposure list.

A separate manifest checksum permits renamed or copied package directories. It detects ordinary manifest changes, while saved readers retain the identity observed when opened. It does not prevent coordinated changes to both data and checksums. Accept duplicated small artifacts and history in exchange for independent copies. Validation proves supplied-record consistency and hash integrity, not complete disclosure or immutable storage. Private publication permissions and strict directory contents remain intentional. Crash durability, silent repair, cross-account authorization, and multi-gigabyte streaming are outside this first byte-oriented API. [Project state](project-state.md) records verification progress.

Opening validates the root listing, complete artifact-directory listing, manifest, and every referenced artifact. A retained reader rechecks the root listing, opened manifest identity, and requested artifact bytes. It does not repeat the complete artifact-directory listing check on each read. The record returned by `inspect` is a presentation, not a canonical manifest export.

Manifest schema version 2 replaced the single caller-declared coverage value on 22 October 2026. A package's `disclosure_coverage` is `declared_inputs` when its producer asserts that the exposure events cover everything it read from the inputs its trials declare. It is `unknown` when the producer makes no such claim, which is the default. Version 1 accepted only `caller_declared`, which made no completeness claim, so readers open version 1 packages as `unknown`. Saving writes version 2, or version 3 for a record that selects no trial, per [D060](#d060-let-a-package-record-no-selected-trial). The value changes vocabulary rather than the key set, and a version 1 reader refuses version 2 by its number rather than misreading a value. Opening rejects a value outside the manifest version's vocabulary. The format cannot check the claim itself, because it does not interpret artifact contents.

Canonical serialization owns UTF-8 conversion and translates only its encoding failure into `PackageError`. Specification hashing reaches this boundary before field parsing, so per-field encoding alone missed malformed specification text. Remove the discarded per-field encoding rather than adding a second error owner. Ten public save cases cover high and low surrogates across specification, environment, outcome, exposure, and artifact text. Valid Unicode retains its pre-fix canonical bytes and specification identity; unrelated serialization errors still propagate.

The hand-authored development example creates a new trial on every invocation. A local probe found that it reused a fixed trial ID across different lock-derived specifications. Keep the committed example frozen as historical evidence while correcting future authoring. Preserve the source archive, frozen reviewed plan, and saved example bytes through Git line-ending conversion. Hashes refer to those bytes rather than reformatted copies.

## D007. Install the Git boundary and execute the selected snapshot's policy

Selected during the first foundation review on 21 October 2026. Keep one authored runner, install its Git entry points in the common Git directory, and execute dependency policy and suites from each captured snapshot under its exact Python pin. A second design and independent judge preferred this shape over a separate launcher module. A separate module would split capture and policy changes across authored files. The installed-copy design keeps the capture owner stable while selecting dependency policy from the target. The advisor accepted it with corrections to private dispatch, result decoding, component traversal, and atomic installation.

Installed gates must ignore ordinary unstaged runner and Makefile edits. The installed outer command owns capture, safe export, outgoing refs, and publication. The selected worker owns dependency configuration, runtime identity, matching preparation, and suite behavior. A commit with different dependency policy must not inherit the checked-out branch's rules. Keep the existing permanent dependency generations. Captured application source takes precedence over dependency modules, with a real tools package marker. Verification tools are the exception. Import pytest and its helper package from prepared dependencies before exposing captured paths, and do the same for coverage. Coverage resolves pytest later, so loading coverage alone leaves that target exposed. Packages already imported with those tools remain bound in Python's module cache. Ordinary captured root and source modules retain precedence, as the import regression verifies.

Require that marker in every captured snapshot before launching its worker. Merely committing it in the current branch does not protect an outgoing commit that removes it. Keep inherited coverage settings outside the child environment so coverage uses the captured configuration. Coverage regressions add their ABI-specific dependency and regenerate the lock only in fixtures that consume it; generic fixtures retain both pinned runtime paths. Bind GitHub CLI calls to github.com, matching the accepted remote, and exhaust qualified-head API pages while checking full head repository identity. A branch-name-only lookup can mistake a fork for the local head or miss a later duplicate. Title validation rejects conventional breaking-change prefixes and NUL before any GitHub lookup. Pass the captured push URL directly to Git alongside the captured object ID, preserving normal hooks. Re-resolving the remote name after verification allowed a concurrent push-URL edit to change the destination. This freezes that selected URL, not other Git transport configuration or URL rewrite rules.

Two architecture sketches and a separate judge selected a persistent common-directory advisory lock for installation. Private staging and compilation remain separate. Read backups only after acquiring the lock, then retain it through publication, Git configuration, and rollback. The shared Git installation is a real invariant; relocating code cannot coordinate independent processes. Immutable bundles with pinned readers are a viable stronger alternative, but require entrypoint migration and generation retention. This lock serializes writers only. It does not give unlocked concurrent readers a single bundle or provide crash recovery. Windows and network-filesystem behavior remain unverified.

Validate exported symlinks from the complete Git-entry table before creating them. Resolve components in order without consulting outside files. Preserve confined internal and dangling links, raw targets, and modes. Reject absolute targets, any excursion outside the root, cycles, and regular files used as directories.

The portable naming profile rejects case-folded and canonical-Unicode collisions among distinct tracked paths and ancestor directories. Link targets must not use a differently spelled alias of a known entry. This avoids a link that is dangling on one filesystem but resolves outside the export on another. It is a conservative cross-platform profile, not a complete model of every filesystem's Unicode behavior.

Accept one subprocess handoff, a fixed bootstrap syntax baseline, explicit installation refresh, and rejection of snapshots without the private worker contract. Copied or moved repositories must reinstall hooks. Explicit setup may use its working installer; a manual Make command still needs its Makefile to parse. Website/API operations and bypassed or uninstalled hooks remain outside enforcement.

The [retained source-boundary checkpoint](https://github.com/sunpar/quant-research/commit/a61c1e47ef6c8c599795a38173d3843d146a88c5) passed 83 tests through the normal push hook. The later native Muse review found the naming, source-kind, installed-content, and GitHub-response gaps addressed in this revision. Earlier trail references to private `/tmp` artifacts and uncommitted tree IDs are local historical observations. They cannot substantiate a fresh checkout, and this consolidated rationale does not retroactively reproduce them. Use the retained code, repository commands, current test cases, and the current [project-state evidence](project-state.md) for reproducible checks.

The corrected 160-case exact-index checkpoint preserved all existing generations, index bytes, tracked files, and installed hooks. Later cleanup removed duplicate path traversals, link state, unused outgoing reports, and fixture response branches. Corruption fixtures replace installed files atomically because uv may hardlink module and metadata bytes to other environments or its cache. An isolated hardlink probe demonstrated that in-place writes could change a sibling; atomic replacement preserved it. The resulting focused 103-case check passed. Final outgoing verification and independent delivery evidence apply to their actual committed revisions.

A full supported publication command exposed inherited Make input in four nested default-base test cases. Those cases now pass the fixture request's empty base explicitly, alongside its existing head, title, and body. This corrects test ownership without changing legal Make inheritance or adding environment guards. The same eleven lookup cases reproduced four failures before the one-line correction and passed afterward under identical inherited settings.

## D008. Seal fresh generations and reconcile publication

Selected on 22 October 2026 after the final foundation review. A private probe replaced pytest and its RECORD coherently; the old check accepted it and skipped a failing assertion. Fresh locked preparation now records a complete generation fingerprint in an atomic `repo-prepared-v2` receipt. Reuse checks that fingerprint before executing prepared code. Older workers retain their policy; new workers require explicit preparation for unsealed receipts. The [architecture](architecture.md#boundaries-for-the-initial-local-workflow) records coverage and trust limits. Rewriting both the receipt and generation remains outside this boundary.

The PR wrapper rejects heads without commits beyond the resolved base before pushing and matches duplicate PRs by repository, head, and base. It verifies a created PR before separate self-assignment. Partial creation preserves its URL, output, and incomplete status; one qualified lookup can recover a missing URL without retrying creation. Physical capture and export ignore replacement refs after a disposable push demonstrated substitution of passing bytes for a failing raw commit. The history query also ignores legacy grafts and disables lazy fetch.

Existing fixture helpers now install deliberate dependency content before sealing. Later mutation fails without repair. The correction adds lines because independent preparation evidence and partial-publication recovery cannot be established by deleting or relocating the old checks alone. [Project state](project-state.md) records the capability.

Publication retries exposed a bidirectional pipe deadlock during batch Git-object export. The existing subprocess helper now supplies input through a private temporary file, removing that pipe cycle. Final affected public-command and hook results are recorded on the PRs.

## D009. Verify the built application without installing it

Selected for the portable-package increment on 21 October 2026. Build with the prepared locked backend, then run a save/open Python consumer and both CLI commands from the wheel in a fresh directory. Keep source paths absent and the prepared environment unchanged. A source test pass cannot establish that the distribution contains its required modules.

Keep uv's project mode virtual and invoke Hatchling directly. This removes the editable-root policy exception and the unused build frontend. Builder dependencies belong only to fixtures that build a wheel.

The package fixture inserts Hatchling into its own development group and calls the existing relock helper, matching the coverage fixture. A replacement tied to the generic list's trailing comma silently stopped adding the backend after the runtime fixture correction. The correction changes that edit at its consumer. Generated source fixtures also follow the suite that consumes them. The package suite formats `src`, so its inherited shadow fixture uses a double-quoted value and a multiline `Path` call that Ruff preserves for short and long temporary paths. Every behavioral assertion remains unchanged.

The verified distribution is the wheel. Source-distribution file selection is not implemented or verified; a default backend source archive may include private repository material. No source-distribution publication is part of this increment.

Local authoring and inspection execute working source with dependencies selected from one captured index. The captured worker validates that environment without running a test suite. Return its lock bytes while the export remains alive, so example provenance cannot drift to an unstaged lock or a later index. [Architecture](architecture.md) lists the commands; [the initial PR plan](initial-prs.md) retains the release boundaries.

## D010. Replay one declared synthetic causal profile

Selected for the causal replay increment on 22 October 2026. The [replay module](../src/quant_research/replay.py) owns a fixed version 1 synthetic contract, causal input selection, a level-difference signal, and a descriptive Pearson diagnostic. The [visible fixtures](../tests/fixtures/causal/known-answer.json) and [behavioral tests](../tests/test_replay.py) are development cases whose expected values can be derived independently. They are not held-out evaluation tasks or evidence for a real framework.

At each decision, use the membership in the entity's latest known vintage, which [D029](#d029-treat-the-latest-membership-vintage-as-complete) defines, and the highest eligible revision for the current and immediately preceding declared session. The session must have occurred, and the selected version must have been published and must have arrived, at or before the decision. An older revision that arrives late cannot replace a higher eligible revision. Save the selected version keys and the reasons for missing inputs. Labels join signals only during evaluation. Each label must become available after its decision and by the declared evaluation cutoff, so a label known at decision time rejects the fixture. Labels cannot enter signal construction. Observation timestamps are checked against each decision separately, because an embargoed value can arrive before its publication time.

Accept ASCII decimal strings with at most 34 significant digits and stored and adjusted exponents within [-100, 100]. Row parsing, signal subtraction, outcome text, and result serialization share one fixed Decimal context, so the caller's context cannot change saved bytes. Signal subtraction and diagnostic output use precision 34 with half-even rounding. A syntax rejection is distinct from a significant-digit or exponent rejection. Internal centered moments and their products are exact at a working precision derived from those input limits. Inputs lie on the 10^-100 grid, and level differences stay below 10^102, so scaled pair coordinates stay below 10^202. With d decimal digits in the pair count N, the product bound is below 4 * N^4 * 10^808. The working precision is 4 * (202 + d) + 16, which keeps 15 digits beyond the sufficient bound.

For centered cross moment C and variance moments Vx and Vy, the coefficient is sign(C) * sqrt(C^2 / (Vx * Vy)). Division and square root round in two precision-34 steps, so the result is not the once-rounded exact Pearson coefficient. The saved centered cross and variance fields are centered sums of products and squares, not sample covariances or variances. Fewer than three eligible pairs and zero variance are distinct inconclusive outcomes. An exact zero coefficient is a null result. A nonzero coefficient is a descriptive calculation, not evidence of a relationship.

A fixture must be strict JSON with unique object keys and no bare nonfinite constants. A document that fails this check or declares an unsupported contract creates no trial. Malformed row values in an admitted document produce a rejected attempt that keeps the raw input and has no coefficient. NewTrial, Repeat, and Retry stay separate intents. A repeat adds a deliberate run to an identical specification. A retry names an existing attempt, keeps its run, and records it as the predecessor. A changed input, contract, implementation, lock, environment, or question requires a new trial linked to the supplied history. Every supplied trial, attempt, artifact, and disclosure travels with the result, and conflicting supplied identities fail.

Each attempt saves eight artifact roles. They are the raw input, the contract, the source of every module in the package, the caller-supplied lock, selected-version provenance, signals, evaluation pairs, and the diagnostic. Because the implementation identity includes the CLI source, any source change requires a linked new trial. The attempt start is observed before the fixture is read, software data exposure after the read, and result exposure after computation. These events establish neither human exposure nor complete historical disclosure. When the fixture parses, both events span every timestamp in the raw input. That includes rows replay does not select, such as later revisions, excluded memberships, and labels available after the cutoff. Reading a whole fixture exposes its whole span, including the gaps between rows, so each event keeps one interval rather than one event per row. The package then records `declared_inputs` coverage, provided every supplied history package also records it. A rejected fixture stops parsing at its first malformed row. Its events keep the declared calendar and evaluation span, and the package records `unknown` coverage, as does a package whose supplied history has unknown coverage. Caller-supplied disclosures add events and leave coverage unchanged. Replay keeps the lock as caller-supplied evidence and does not install it or reconcile the environment. The runtime record holds the major, minor, and patch components of `sys.version_info` with the observed ABI and platform. It does not record prerelease or build provenance, and the uv version is recorded as unobserved.

The wheel consumer writes its own small fixture and lock file, because full verification copies only the consumer and the example package into its directory. It runs the replay command from the built wheel and checks the signals, coefficient, and saved module source.

Accept copied small artifacts and an intentionally fixed fixture profile in exchange for a portable local consumer. Do not add portfolio construction, execution simulation, an engine adapter, statistical significance claims, or confirmation outcomes. [Open questions](open-questions.md) still control the next real integration.

## D011. Seal capability-evaluation answers and retire disclosed tasks

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the [capability-evaluation protocol](master-plan.md#how-progress-will-be-judged). Each task-set version has two files. The tasks file holds the inputs that a procedure may read. A separate answer key holds each task's kind, expected estimates, and tolerance, and names the digest of its task set. The kind belongs in the answer key because it reveals the expected outcome. A procedure is a plain Python callable that receives a fresh copy of one task's inputs, without the task name or answer. A run therefore needs no answer key, and one run cannot change the inputs of a later run. The run accepts only an exact response object with a known status and finite numeric estimates under text names. It checks exact built-in types on a snapshot, so an accepted response is plain data, and it records that snapshot before disclosure sees it. The loaders reject repeated JSON keys, which would otherwise replace an answer silently, and values that canonical JSON cannot encode, such as NaN or a lone surrogate.

A ledger freezes a comparison before its outcomes are disclosed. The comparison identity covers the task-set digest, the answer key's digest, the inputs digest of each held-out task, every configuration, and the repeat count. Freeze takes only the key's digest, so a run still needs no answer key. Each held-out task keeps the key of the first comparison frozen over it. Freeze refuses that task under another key, even in a copied task-set version, so responses already seen cannot be scored against a second key. Freeze also refuses more than 1,000 repeats, because running and disclosing build every configuration, task, and repeat at once. A configuration records its implementation, model versions, starting memory, resources, and human steering. Disclosure is the only operation that scores results. It requires a frozen comparison, the answer key whose digest that comparison committed, and each configuration, task, and repeat run exactly once. Results therefore cannot omit or duplicate a run, and disclosure rejects a key edited after its comparison was frozen. Disclosure returns one score per run and exposes the comparison's tasks. Each task's outcomes are disclosed once. A later freeze excludes exposed tasks, and a comparison frozen earlier over an exposed task cannot be disclosed, because its procedures could have changed after the outcomes were known. A frozen baseline therefore runs beside new configurations in one comparison. Freeze and disclosure both reject a disclosed configuration name with a different identity, so a frozen baseline keeps one meaning.

Exposure follows the inputs digest of each task rather than its name. A new task that reuses a name stays held out, and an exposed task copied under a new name stays exposed. The ledger cannot detect an edited copy of an exposed task. Scoring reuses the package outcome vocabulary. Known-answer and paper-reproduction tasks need a successful outcome with every expected estimate within tolerance. A null task accepts null or inconclusive, the outcomes that make no positive claim in the Phase 4 gate. A flawed task needs rejected.

Amended on 22 October 2026 to commit the answer key and keep the ledger in a local file store before the registry exists, as [D018](#d018-publish-complete-source-generations-from-a-file-store) does for sources. The store is an existing directory of events, each a canonical JSON file named by its position from `0.json`. A freeze event holds the comparison. A disclosure event holds the comparison ID and a digest of the scored results. Each file is created complete under its number and never rewritten. Every operation first replays all events through the same transitions that admitted them, so ledgers opened on one directory never act on stale state. Replay rejects non-canonical bytes, unknown fields or schema versions, gaps, stray files other than hidden (dot-prefixed) files such as the temporary files an interrupted write leaves, a comparison frozen twice, over disclosed tasks, or over a held-out task under another key, a disclosure of an unfrozen comparison or of disclosed tasks, and a configuration name bound to two identities. When two writers claim the same number with different events, the second rereads the store and checks its event again. A concurrent disclosure of other results for the same comparison therefore fails as already disclosed.

Retirement holds for every ledger opened on the same directory. A ledger opened on another or an empty directory knows no earlier disclosure, and the ledger cannot detect deleted trailing events, an appended well-formed event, or a consistent rewrite of the store. Writes are not synced to disk. A process that stops after recording a disclosure and before returning its scores cannot disclose that comparison again, and the ledger keeps only a digest of the scored results, not the scores. The registry should import these events. A configuration declares its implementation, and the ledger cannot verify the callable's code or tell whether the results it receives came from a single run. A task's key binds only its exact inputs, so an edited copy of a frozen task can be frozen under another key. A freeze under a wrong key digest binds its tasks to that digest, so no later freeze can use the right key for them. A procedure runs in the evaluating process, so the seal covers the call interface, not introspection of that process or code the procedure leaves running there, such as threads or finalizers. The synthetic task set in the tests exercises the mechanism only. Real tasks and answers need the Phase 0 policy that [the causal replay plan](initial-prs.md#pr-3-replay-a-causal-development-fixture) requires. Aggregate statistics belong to the evaluation profile.

## D012. Record model provenance and role versions as inactive data

Selected on 22 October 2026 for Phases 2 and 4 of the [master plan](master-plan.md). [The model provenance module](../src/quant_research/model_provenance.py) records each model by provider, model identifier, version, and knowledge cutoff. An unknown version or cutoff is the explicit `UNKNOWN` value, never a default date. Identity encodes `UNKNOWN` as JSON null, so no date or version text has the same bytes. The module is not named `models` because that name also suggests generic data models.

A role version is a snapshot of a campaign's model roles. It binds a plain campaign identifier to each role's primary model, its ordered fallbacks, and a reason. Its identity is a SHA-256 digest of the package module's canonical JSON, and it includes the identity of the version it replaces. A change to an assignment, a fallback, or a model version creates a new linked version and leaves the prior version unchanged. A run can then cite one identity and read every role without replaying changes. We rejected an event log of assignment changes. A comparison of two snapshots shows the kind of change, and the reason records the intent.

Role versions declare `activation` as `inactive`, as packages declare confirmation unavailable. They hold no credentials and load no network or provider code. Model roles activate only after Phase 3 provider acceptance. Campaign specifications and provider data policy remain outside this record, which names campaigns and providers by plain identifiers.

A cutoff check compares one model with one historical evaluation period in inclusive dates. A period that starts after the cutoff returns `period_after_cutoff`. A period that starts on or before the cutoff returns `period_before_or_overlapping_cutoff`, and an unknown cutoff returns `cutoff_unknown`. Each status supplies the sentence that a report prints. The two flagged sentences state that the model may already contain knowledge of the period even when the platform withheld its results.

Cutoffs are provider statements that the platform does not verify. Record a month-level statement as the last day of that month. An after-cutoff status depends on the stated cutoff, and its sentence names the cutoff as recorded. Two validations reject ambiguous input, a role assigned twice in one version and a period that ends before it starts. Text that is not valid UTF-8 raises the package module's `PackageError`, a `ValueError`, because identity reuses its canonical serializer. A stored knowledge cutoff other than null or canonical ISO 8601 date text raises `PackageError` naming the field. The error never quotes the value and chains nothing, because Python's date parser quotes its input. A [D048](#d048-review-restricted-confirmation-reports-through-audited-grants) report placed in that field therefore leaves no outcome in the replay error of a provider call log. Runs and reports do not consume these records yet. [To dos](todos.md) tracks that step.

## D013. Version campaign specifications as content-addressed revisions

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the first Phase 2 campaign increment. A campaign specification binds a question to its sources, permitted development data, evaluation rules, resource limits, intervention points, and model roles. Its `generated_code` section is false unless the campaign enables generated Python, the separately gated extension that [D047](#d047-run-generated-signal-code-in-a-locked-down-container) runs. The stored file holds the section only when it is true, so revisions written before the section existed keep their bytes and identities. [quant_research.campaigns](../src/quant_research/campaigns.py) stores each revision as one canonical JSON file named by the SHA-256 of its bytes. A revision names its parent's identity, so an edit to an earlier revision changes its name and breaks every child link. `revise` returns a new revision and never changes its parent's object or file.

Each revision records the complete specification, the sections that differ from its parent, and the trials and exposure events observed before it. A child's trial references and exposures must begin with its parent's, so a revision cannot drop earlier exposure. The check applies to files written by hand, not only to `revise` output. Observation belongs to the whole campaign, so a revision must also include every trial and exposure that any earlier revision recorded, including revisions on other branches. Otherwise a sibling branch could present a change as unexposed although the campaign had already seen the evidence. Each trial ID and exposure ID names one record across the campaign, so saving or opening rejects a revision that records an ID differently. An exposure dated after its revision is rejected. A revision that changes `data` or `evaluation` after observation therefore lists that change beside the exposure it carries. A new campaign accepts prior trials and exposures for the same reason. A revision records no coverage value, so its exposure history makes no completeness claim. Packages record one, as [D006](#d006-keep-development-packages-self-contained) describes. Revision times are caller-declared too, so a revision dated at or before another branch's recording revision can omit what that revision recorded. Saving does not guard against this backdating.

A branch is a named linear chain inside the revision graph. Saving rejects a second revision of one parent on the same branch, and a new branch must use an unused name. The researcher creates a branch instead of a fork. This rule is a project choice, not a stated requirement, and it gives every branch one current head. Saving validates the whole stored history before it writes. Like `save_package`, saving takes no lock, so two concurrent writers can race.

Records reuse the package module's canonical JSON, identifier, timestamp, digest, and exposure parsers. Those helpers are private to `packages.py`, which this increment does not change. A later refactor moved the campaign field check into `records.parse_fields` and deleted the campaign copy, so every caller of that parser names the missing and unknown fields. Branch and source names likewise use `records.parse_name`, so campaigns and packages accept the same names. Model roles and intervention points are data only. Roles stay inactive until Phase 3 provider acceptance, and no runner reads intervention points yet. Validation does not resolve source or evaluation digests to bytes. It also does not check exposures against permitted data intervals, because prior exposure on data that a later protocol no longer permits must survive.

The rejected alternative was one append-only history file per campaign. Each append changes that file's content identity, so the file cannot identify an immutable revision. There is no storage engine or campaign CLI command, and the [D042](#d042-run-each-campaign-as-one-temporal-workflow-that-owns-its-logs) runner reads one revision. A SurrealDB registry can import these files later without regenerating them, per [the SurrealDB plan](surrealdb-plan.md).

## D014. Fit and label within walk-forward windows

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the Phase 1 requirement that preprocessing is fitted within training windows. [quant_research.splits](../src/quant_research/splits.py) owns evaluation windows and fitting discipline. Causal replay owns fixture timing ([D010](#d010-replay-one-declared-synthetic-causal-profile)), and the evaluation statistics own their measures. This module duplicates neither.

The data shape is a strictly increasing index of decision times. A sample's features use observations through its own position. Its label reads observations through its position plus a declared horizon, counted in index steps. Rows and observations align with the index by position. Evaluation windows tile forward from a configured start, and training expands from the first position. A fold exists only when the index contains every observation its evaluation labels read. Appending observations therefore adds folds and never moves earlier ones. Windows measured in time durations were the alternative. They need calendar rules that Phase 0 has not supplied, and position tables are easier to check by hand.

Purging removes every training sample whose label reaches the evaluation window. The embargo differs from the one in purged k-fold validation, which withholds samples after each evaluation window. In walk-forward validation each model trains only on samples before its own evaluation window, so that form would remove nothing. This embargo withholds a further fixed number of samples before the purged ones. It covers dependence that the label horizon does not declare, such as serial correlation or a misrecorded publication lag. Horizon and embargo stay separate settings because the labeler declares the horizon and the researcher chooses the embargo.

A fitting step receives a read-only view of its fold's training rows. Subscripting any other position raises `LeakageError`, and `get` returns its default. The label check computes labels from all observations, then again from only the observations each fold's training labels may read. A training label that changes or disappears raises `LeakageError`. This catches a label that reaches past its declared horizon and one normalized with full-sample statistics. The check's boundary ends before the embargo, so an embargo cannot hide an understated horizon.

The module sees only the data passed through it. A step or labeler that reads a captured copy of the full dataset bypasses both checks. Applying fitted parameters to evaluation rows is the caller's responsibility. The label check assumes a deterministic labeler. It counts two `NaN` labels at the same position as equal, also inside tuples, lists, mappings, sets, and dataclasses at any depth, and a `NaN` against a number or an absent label as a change. Containers compare as plain tuples, lists, and dicts, so a subclass's own equality and a mapping's key order do not count. A set compares by its members and how often each matches, so a set with two `NaN` members differs from one with a single `NaN`. A dataclass compares by its class and its compared fields, so, as with a container subclass, its own `__eq__` does not count. A tolerance `__eq__` therefore counts a small change as a change, and attributes outside the fields are not compared. A label whose comparison is elementwise, such as a numpy array with one or more dimensions, raises `LeakageError`, because the check cannot match it. Supporting arrays would need numpy, which the project does not depend on, or a conversion rule that the tests cannot exercise, so a caller converts an array label to a tuple or list. numpy scalars compare as single values and are checked. Two labels whose comparison with each other is elementwise at any depth, such as a numpy scalar against a list, count as a change. A random label usually changes when recomputed, so the check reports it as a leak. Rolling training windows, time-based horizons, and per-sample horizons are not implemented. The tests use small synthetic indexes and do not establish real-framework acceptance.

## D015. Evaluate pilot signals against their recorded trials

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the pilot evaluation profile in [Phase 1 of the master plan](master-plan.md#phase-1-reproduce-a-trusted-experiment). [quant_research.evaluation](../src/quant_research/evaluation.py) takes one return per non-overlapping period, an optional baseline on the same periods, and the package record that holds the search history. It returns a supported, null, or inconclusive verdict with its intervals, or an invalid-input reason. An honest null or inconclusive verdict is a successful evaluation.

The tested effect is the mean return. With a baseline, it is the intercept of returns regressed on the baseline. That intercept is the return that linear exposure to the baseline does not explain. It makes no claim about a combined portfolio, and the baseline must be chosen before outcomes are seen. Robustness splits the series into contiguous subperiods and requires a positive estimate of the tested effect in each.

Selection uses a Bonferroni level. Each interval's confidence is one minus alpha divided by the number of trials in the record. Runs and retry attempts within a trial add nothing, and failed trials count. Bonferroni holds under any dependence among trials and is conservative when trials are correlated variants. A deflated Sharpe ratio needs each trial's statistic, and packages record generic outcomes instead. The count is only as complete as the caller-declared history, so unrecorded exploration makes the threshold too lenient. Counting also assumes the set of trials did not depend on their outcomes. Stopping a search after a favorable result can exceed alpha. Exposure events do not become trials.

One declared threshold, the minimum effect worth having, decides the verdict. The result is null when the adjusted interval lies below it, including an interval entirely between zero and the minimum. It is supported when the interval lies above it and every subperiod estimate is positive. Otherwise it is inconclusive. The minimum effect must be positive. At zero, rounding noise decided the verdict for a candidate that was an exact scaled copy of its baseline. The null rule uses the same widened interval, so a null verdict becomes rarer as the trial count grows. Selection already biases the chosen estimate upward, so this errs toward caution. Each interval keeps its standard error, so a reader can recompute the unadjusted interval and the size of the selection adjustment.

Intervals use Student's t quantile with T - 1 degrees of freedom for the mean and T - 2 for the intercept. The standard library has no t quantile, so the module uses the expansion in Abramowitz and Stegun's *Handbook of Mathematical Functions*, formula 26.7.5 (page 949). At alpha 0.05, its tail level was within 0.1% of nominal for 28 or more degrees of freedom and up to 100,000 trials, measured against numerical integration. For Gaussian returns, a normal quantile would have doubled the per-trial level at 30 observations and 20 trials. The standard library's `NormalDist` and `linear_regression` supply the rest, so the profile adds no dependency.

The intervals assume independent, identically distributed returns, and for the intercept, independent errors with constant variance around a linear relation. They are exact for Gaussian returns and approximate otherwise, so family-wise error control is approximate too. The profile requires at least 30 observations and two per subperiod. Zero variance is invalid input because it yields no interval. Replay reports the same condition as inconclusive, so a module that adopts this profile maps between the two. The minimum effect, subperiod count, and alpha must be declared before evaluation. Each result records them with the procedure version, per [D028](#d028-keep-replay-descriptive-and-record-evaluation-parameters-with-each-result), and package specifications do not. The profile makes no portfolio, execution, or real-data claim. The researcher, who holds the methodological reviewer role under [D050](#d050-run-the-phase-0-pilot-locally-on-public-us-macro-data), accepted the profile and its P04 parameters on 23 October 2026, per [D055](#d055-run-the-reference-experiment-through-one-audited-pipeline-against-a-frozen-experiment-file). Its inference still rests on the assumptions above.

## D016. Account for resources in an append-only ledger

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the runtime resource accounting in Phases 2 and 7 of the [master plan](master-plan.md). [quant_research.accounting](../src/quant_research/accounting.py) keeps an append-only list of events. An event reserves capacity for an attempt, charges usage to it, or releases it with its outcome. Every budget check, stall, and report reads a fold of that list, so no second record of spend can disagree with it. The fold advances by the events appended since the previous call. It keeps each campaign's released spend and its open attempts' charges, so an exposure check sums only the open attempts, and each run's count of open and failed attempts, so a failure-slot check is one lookup. A refused or failed call writes into a throwaway overlay of the fold and leaves no trace. [D073](#d073-bound-the-campaign-runners-per-event-cost-and-payloads) records the measurements.

Each usage field names its unit: compute seconds, model calls, input tokens, output tokens, and US dollars. Seconds and dollars use `Decimal`. The ledger rejects floats, booleans, negative amounts, and non-finite amounts. One currency avoids a conversion policy that nobody has chosen.

Work reuses the trial, run, and attempt identities of [portable packages](../src/quant_research/packages.py). A run belongs to one trial, and a trial belongs to one campaign. Each charge names its attempt, so a retry charges its own attempt inside the same run and trial.

A campaign's exposure is the charges of its released attempts plus, for each open attempt, the larger of its estimate and its charges. Without that maximum, an attempt that overran its estimate would free capacity that it already spent. The ledger refuses a reservation when exposure plus the estimate would pass any declared limit, including a sum too large to represent exactly. The refusal names each limit with its committed, requested, and declared amounts. A sum too large to represent exactly on a resource without a declared limit raises an error and appends nothing. A refusal takes precedence over that error. The ledger raises it only for a reservation that every declared limit admits, so a refused reservation returns its refusals even when the sum of an undeclared resource is too large. The same rule controls repeated failure. Each failed or open attempt holds one of its run's failure slots, so overlapping retries cannot pass the declared limit. When the slots are full, the ledger refuses the next retry. A run whose failed attempts alone reach the limit is reported as a stall. A repeat has a new run ID and starts with no failures.

A repeated reservation, charge, or release with identical content appends nothing, and conflicting content raises an error. A refused reservation also appends nothing, so a retry evaluates current spend again. A charge requires an earlier reservation, so all spend passes an admission decision. The ledger never refuses a charge for exceeding a budget, and it accepts late billing after release. It rejects a charge that would make the campaign's exposure impossible to sum exactly, since exposure bounds every later sum for the campaign. Reports sum charges exactly by campaign, role, and outcome, and they list spend on unreleased attempts as open. Usage arithmetic runs in the module's own `Decimal` context with maximum precision and raises instead of rounding. That context sets every field instead of inheriting `decimal.DefaultContext`, so neither the caller's context nor the default context at import can change a sum or a refusal.

Campaign specifications own the declared limits, and the ledger accepts them as plain values. A stall here counts failed attempts; detecting a job that makes no progress belongs to the execution layer.

`LedgerStore` keeps the events in an existing local directory, as the [capability exposure ledger](#d011-seal-capability-evaluation-answers-and-retire-disclosed-tasks) does. Event *i* is the canonical JSON file `i.json`, created once by linking a complete temporary file into place. Replay ignores the hidden temporary files that an interrupted write leaves. UUIDs use their canonical text and `Decimal` amounts their exact string, so replay restores the same values. Every operation first reads its new event back as replay reads it. An event that the next open would refuse, such as one whose work is a stand-in object instead of a `Work`, raises `LedgerError` and stores nothing. The error names a rejected UUID or outcome by its field, as in `Invalid run_id UUID`, and never quotes it. The operation then replays the stored events into a fresh in-memory ledger through `reserve`, `charge`, and `release`, applies the event it read back, and writes any new event under the next number. Each stored event must append exactly one event when replayed. A refused, repeated, or conflicting event, an event placed before the reservation it needs, a gap, a stray file, or noncanonical bytes therefore fail the open. When another writer has taken the number, the operation replays and decides again, so concurrent writers on one directory admit only reservations that fit together. Refused and repeated requests write nothing. Replay reads, parses, and folds each stored event once. Each reservation or charge also sums the open attempts of its campaign, so its cost grows with the number of attempts open at that point. Decimal amounts must keep their digits between `1E-999999` and `1E+999999`, so an exact sum never allocates without bound.

Until 22 October 2026, the check that each replayed event appended one event also copied every event before it, so a load grew with the square of the event count. Other sessions loaded the 14-core development machine near 350 during these measurements. In these loads, each attempt was reserved, charged, and released before the next one opened. Five loads of each size took this much CPU time before and after that change:

- 3,000 events took 0.28 to 0.35 seconds before and 0.27 to 0.37 seconds after, so a few thousand events show no difference.
- 30,000 events took 5.0 to 5.8 seconds before and 3.5 to 3.8 seconds after.
- 60,000 events took 30 to 37 seconds before and 9.9 to 19 seconds after.

The ledger sums the open attempts on each check instead of keeping their sum in the fold, because no caller holds many attempts of one campaign open at once. The [D042](#d042-run-each-campaign-as-one-temporal-workflow-that-owns-its-logs) runner reserves, charges, and releases each attempt before it reserves the next. The [D041](#d041-check-outbound-provider-content-against-the-run-and-a-versioned-provider-policy) gateway charges attempts and checks that they are open, but it never reserves one, and nothing else reserves through `LedgerStore`. No orchestrator runs the [D017](#d017-define-research-workflows-as-validated-versioned-templates) templates yet, and the five templates it defines authorize at most five branch instances in all. Under loads of 13 to 20, four in-memory runs measured the CPU time of one attempt's reservation, charge, and release:

- With no other attempt open, it took 0.08 to 0.09 milliseconds.
- With 10 attempts open, it took 0.32 to 0.33 milliseconds.
- With 100 attempts open, it took 2.4 to 2.5 milliseconds, close to the 3 milliseconds that Temporal's replay costs per attempt in [D073](#d073-bound-the-campaign-runners-per-event-cost-and-payloads).

The same runs took 7.7 to 9.5 seconds to reserve 1,000 attempts and leave them all open. A reservation that is never released stays open. A caller that crashes between a reservation and its release therefore adds to every later check of its campaign. Keeping each campaign's open exposure in the fold waits for a caller that holds tens of attempts open at once or leaves them unreleased.

The store holds no budgets. The caller supplies them at every open, and a store opens only under budgets that name every recorded campaign and admit its recorded reservations. Lowering a limit below what was already admitted therefore fails the open instead of rewriting history. Any edit that still passes replay goes undetected, such as a changed charge amount, deleted trailing events, reordered independent events, or an appended admissible event, and writes are not synced to disk. The store has no runner, provider pricing, or cancellation of running work. The [Temporal qualification](#d026-qualify-temporal-for-durable-campaign-execution) checks its integer budget inside workflow code, which must not perform I/O, so the [D042](#d042-run-each-campaign-as-one-temporal-workflow-that-owns-its-logs) runner charges an in-memory ledger in workflow state instead of this store, and enforcement through the real compute path waits for [Q005](open-questions.md#q005-which-infrastructure-and-provider-assumptions-are-correct). Registry import waits for the SurrealDB registry.

## D017. Define research workflows as validated, versioned templates

Selected for Phase 6 on 22 October 2026. [quant_research.workflows](../src/quant_research/workflows.py) defines a research workflow template as frozen data and validates it without executing it. The later orchestrator receives a fixed contract, and researchers can compare configurations by identity before any multi-branch run exists. A general visual editor waits for demonstrated demand, as the [master plan](master-plan.md#phase-6-add-configurable-swarms-and-research-workflows) allows.

A `WorkflowTemplate` declares roles with their tools, branches with their steps, shared resources, evidence exchanges, and human checkpoints. Each step belongs to one branch, names one declared role, and receives that role's tools. Step dependencies form one acyclic graph across all branches. One iteration runs a branch instance's steps in dependency order, and `max_iterations` caps the iterations of each instance. `max_instances` caps how many copies of a branch the orchestrator may authorize. Instances of one branch are identical, so the orchestrator assigns each instance its focus or source when it authorizes the instance. A dependency on a step in another branch waits for every authorized instance of that branch. A checkpoint holds work after its step completes until a human reviews the step. It holds the step's dependents, the next iteration of the step's branch, and the end of the workflow.

An exchange names a source branch, a target branch, and the evidence that flows between them. Every dependency on another branch needs a covering exchange, so the declared exchanges include every flow of evidence that the dependencies imply. An exchange without a dependency shares evidence without blocking. An exchange from a branch to itself lets the instances of that branch share evidence. `independent-replication` declares no such exchange for its replicators, so each replication stays independent until reconciliation.

Validation rejects cyclic, repeated, or unknown dependencies, branch and iteration limits that are not positive integers, steps without a declared role, exchanges that name an undeclared branch, a second exchange from one branch to another, cross-branch dependencies without an exchange, checkpoints after a missing step, a second checkpoint after one step, duplicate or malformed names, a value that is not a string where a name belongs, blank evidence or reasons, text that contains a NUL character, collections that are not tuples, a collection entry of the wrong type, such as a string or `None` among the roles, text without a canonical identity, and a version that disagrees with its previous link. Each `WorkflowError` names the offending element.

The template ID is the SHA-256 digest of the canonical JSON that [packages](../src/quant_research/packages.py) uses for specification IDs. `revise` validates the template it revises. It rejects a change that names an unknown field, or `version` or `previous`, which it sets itself, and a revision that changes nothing. Otherwise it increments the version and records the previous template ID, so each version commits to its predecessor. `differences` lists the declared fields that differ between two templates. That comparison is structural. The Phase 6 outcome comparison against the single-branch baseline waits for execution, then runs through the [capability evaluation](#d011-seal-capability-evaluation-answers-and-retire-disclosed-tasks).

The module holds five templates as Python data. `single-branch` is the Phase 4 baseline. `competing-hypotheses`, `independent-replication`, `focused-critiques`, and `parallel-evidence-searches` are the initial multi-branch patterns. Roles, tools, resources, and limits are plain values. [Campaign specifications](#d013-version-campaign-specifications-as-content-addressed-revisions), [model provenance and role records](#d012-record-model-provenance-and-role-versions-as-inactive-data), and [resource accounting](#d016-account-for-resources-in-an-append-only-ledger) own their meaning, and this module imports none of them. Python data keeps the templates in the tested wheel without a loader or package-data rules. A file format for researcher-authored templates waits for a store or console that needs one.

## D018. Publish complete source generations from a file store

Selected on 22 October 2026 for Phase 3 evidence ingestion. [quant_research.sources](../src/quant_research/sources.py) gives each [layered identity](surrealdb-plan.md#layered-identity) its own record before a database exists. The later SurrealDB registry imports these records instead of redefining them.

The blob is the SHA-256 digest of the original bytes, stored once. A source occurrence hashes the complete caller declaration: media type, access domain, location, revision, acquisition, rights, restriction labels, provider approvals, `published_at`, `source_available_at` with its evidence, and `received_at`. The occurrence record binds that ID to one blob digest. Identical bytes from two sources therefore keep two occurrences and one blob. A second acquisition with a different `received_at` is a second occurrence. The same declaration with different bytes raises `IdentityConflict` before its bytes are stored.

An extraction generation names the occurrence, its blob digest, and the extractor. A chunk generation names that extraction and the chunker. Each version label names an implementation and its configuration. The generation ID derives from these declared inputs; the manifest digest identifies its content. Publication stages the complete manifest privately and renames it into place. A retry with identical content returns the existing generation. Different content under the same declared identity raises `IdentityConflict`. A new extractor or chunker version creates a new generation, and the old one stays readable.

Each generation also records `recorded_at`, the UTC clock reading of its first successful publication, in a `publication.json` file beside the manifest. The [SurrealDB plan](surrealdb-plan.md#4-time-and-causal-eligibility) requires that production time for replay transformations. The time is outside the manifest and its digest. A retry or a losing concurrent publisher therefore returns the first time, and two stores that publish the same inputs share the generation ID and digest but keep their own times.

The manifest retains the extracted text's digest and code-point length beside the chunks, so a reader can check every chunk offset without the extractor. Publication first binds each extraction ID to that digest and length through an exclusive file creation. Changed extractor output under an unchanged version label therefore conflicts, even when its chunks coincide or it arrives with a new chunker. The read API lists only published generations, checks each against its extraction binding, and returns only those the caller's run context may read ([D036](#d036-filter-source-reads-through-the-trusted-run-context)). It ignores private staging directories and raises on a damaged published generation, so a read failure never looks like an empty result.

These limits are deliberate:

- `recorded_at` comes from the publishing process's clock and is outside `manifest.sha256`. Readers reject a missing or noncanonical timestamp but cannot detect an edited one. Concurrent publishers keep the reading of the one whose rename wins, which may not be the earliest reading.
- A killed registration can leave an occurrence record without its blob. Publishing that occurrence raises `SourceError` until the registration is retried. A killed registration or publication can also leave hidden temporary files and staging directories. Readers ignore them, and nothing removes them. Writes are not synced to disk, so publication survives a killed process but not necessarily a power loss.
- Chunk offsets index the extracted text. For the UTF-8 text and markdown pipeline, that text is the decoded original without newline or Unicode normalization.
- Checksums detect ordinary changes, as in [D006](#d006-keep-development-packages-self-contained), not a coordinated rewrite of content and checksums.
- There is no PDF parser, embedding, vector search, database, or network fetching.

The canonical JSON and parsing helpers this module uses live in a public module, as [D033](#d033-share-record-helpers-through-a-public-records-module) records.

## D019. Admit lessons only through recorded review events

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the review-lifecycle increment, before the SurrealDB store exists. [quant_research.findings](../src/quant_research/findings.py) keeps claims, findings, and lessons as separate immutable record types. Each record has a content-derived ID, a scope, and evidence links that either support or contradict. A record changes status only through an appended proposal, review, or supersession event. The `Ledger` derives every status and every eligibility answer from its event list, so a store can save those events and rebuild the same answers.

A record moves from proposed to reviewed, and only a lesson moves on to admitted. Retracted and superseded are final. A claim or finding stands while it is reviewed, and a lesson stands while it is admitted. A lesson is eligible for reuse while it is admitted and every record reached through its supporting links still stands. Retracting or superseding any of those records removes the lesson from eligibility and leaves the lesson's own history unchanged. A lesson that loses one of several supports also becomes ineligible, because its review relied on the whole set. [D046](#d046-suspend-records-whose-support-an-invalidation-removes) narrows this rule for suspended supports. A new lesson that supersedes it after a fresh review restores eligibility.

Contradicting links stay in the record and do not change eligibility. Weighing them is the reviewer's decision. External evidence IDs, such as artifact digests or source generations, always stand. To withdraw one, retract the records that cite it. [D046](#d046-suspend-records-whose-support-an-invalidation-removes) adds a reversible alternative that suspends them through an invalidation. The module refers to evidence by opaque ID and imports no source-generation code. A declaration event states the [D025](#d025-carry-restrictions-with-material-and-authority-with-the-run) restrictions of one external evidence ID, with a declarer, a time, and a reason. The operator or ingestion appends it, never a record's author, so a record cannot understate what it cites. Repeated declarations of one ID combine through D025's derivation, so a later one can tighten restrictions but never loosen them.

A record's restrictions derive from every external item reached through its supporting and contradicting links, transitively through cited records whatever their status, because its author could read all of them. A record has unknown restrictions when any reached record has no evidence or any reached item has no declaration, and no run may read it. Availability is the latest declared availability, so neither a proposal time nor a declaration time enters it. Records gain no field, so every record ID and every stored log load unchanged, and their lessons stay unreadable to runs until their evidence is declared. Each log prefix has a head, a SHA-256 chain over the canonical JSON of its events that starts from the SHA-256 of the empty string. A head names one prefix of one log, so a run context pins its admitted memory with one ([D025](#d025-carry-restrictions-with-material-and-authority-with-the-run)). `Ledger.admissible_lessons` returns the lessons eligible in the prefix at a context's pin whose restrictions the context may read. `eligible_lessons` stays the reviewers' view and applies no restrictions.

A record can cite only records that already exist, so the evidence links cannot form a cycle and a summary cannot support the record it summarizes. A replacement cannot rest on the record it supersedes through supporting links, which stops a summary built on an old lesson from carrying its replacement. The ledger also rejects relation, origin, outcome, and review-action values outside their declared choices, because a misspelled relation would otherwise drop a support from the eligibility check. A finding records `invalid_implementation` and `rejects_idea` as different outcomes. A correction supersedes the original finding and keeps it in history.

Event times are caller-declared, like package exposure events. The ledger compares event times, scope bounds, and `as_of` cutoffs as instants, so a repeated clock hour in one time zone cannot reorder a log that the registry stores in UTC. The planned store will add its own recorded time. No field holds model confidence, so confidence cannot change a status. The ledger records each reviewer but does not require an independent one. That policy waits for the Phase 0 reviewer roles. The [registry](#d039-register-verified-packages-in-surrealdb-and-keep-their-original-bytes-in-an-object-store) stores the log, and [D049](#d049-expose-evidence-to-agents-through-typed-tools-bound-to-the-run-context) gives agents the admissible lessons.

## D020. Fix the reproduction comparison before results

Selected on 22 October 2026 for Phase 3 paper reproduction and tested on a synthetic paper. [Reproduction specifications](../src/quant_research/reproduction.py) record a paper's implementation choices, its reported targets, a comparison basis, and numerical tolerances. Freezing validates the specification and derives its SHA-256 identity from the canonical JSON that portable packages use. The identity is derived from the current content on every use, as in package specifications. Each result keeps the identity it was recorded under, and classification rejects a result whose identity no longer matches. Once a result exists, the specification rejects every revision, including a tolerance change. Replacing the specification inside a recorded reproduction, or mutating a list it holds, cannot reclassify earlier results.

A choice or target with a value cites at least one passage, equation, table, figure, claim, or code locator. Without a value it names an open question, so the record never invents an input. A located candidate may also carry a question when sources disagree. Questions mark ambiguous, contradictory, missing, or restricted source material. Each question must reach a target directly or through a choice that the target depends on. An affected target is inconclusive whatever its observed value, because a mismatch on unresolved inputs is not evidence against the paper. Missing and non-finite results are also inconclusive. Tolerance checks compare the shortest decimal form of each number exactly, so a value written on an inclusive boundary stays inside it. Each run label is recorded once.

Matched-input and adapted comparisons use separate claim vocabularies. Adapted data yields `reproduced_on_adapted_data` or `not_reproduced_on_adapted_data`, never `reproduced`, and the basis enters the identity. A second design review rejected two earlier shapes. Identity binding alone let a caller widen a tolerance, freeze again, and attach the old values to the new identity. A basis label on the classification alone still let an adapted verdict read as reproduced.

A specification may pin one published locator generation by its ID and content digest. Freezing a pinned specification reads locator generations through the caller's run context, per [D036](#d036-filter-source-reads-through-the-trusted-run-context), and requires the pinned generation with that digest. Every equation, table, figure, and claim locator must equal `LocatorGeneration.ref` of a location in that generation, and the location must have the locator's kind. Freezing refuses an unknown ref, a ref of another generation or locator version, a wrong kind, a changed digest, and a generation the context cannot read, and it returns nothing. An unpublished generation and an unreadable one get the same refusal, so the error leaks no label or availability.

The pin enters the identity. The generation digest commits to every location's text, so the specification stores neither span text nor a per-span checksum. A specification therefore carries no restricted source text past the store's read filter. A later locator version publishes a new generation ID, so it cannot change what a frozen specification compared. Passage and code locators stay opaque, because chunk generations have no ref form and the code index owns code refs.

A specification without a pin freezes unchecked, as every specification did before pins existed. Its identity document omits the pin, so its bytes and SHA-256 stay unchanged. The live paper case must pin its generation. A pinned `Reproduction` holds the locator generation it pins. Its constructor validates the specification, checks the generation's ID and digest against the pin, and checks every equation, table, figure, and claim ref against the generation, so building a reproduction directly or replacing its specification runs the same check as freezing. A pinned specification without its generation is refused. Only freezing reads the generation through a run context. The generation stays outside the reproduction's identity, equality, and repr, but the in-memory record holds its location text, which the freezing run could read, and no export path for a reproduction exists. The local record cannot prove that no result existed before freezing, or stop a caller from starting a new reproduction after seeing results. Packaging each run with its exposure history must supply that evidence. The synthetic paper is a development fixture, not acceptance evidence for the live paper case.

## D021. Gate final confirmation on exposure history and frozen shortlists

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the confirmation rules in Phases 0 and 4 of the [master plan](master-plan.md). [quant_research.confirmation](../src/quant_research/confirmation.py) decides eligibility from a lineage's exposure events alone. A confirmation period is a dataset ID and a closed time interval. Any supplied exposure on that dataset that overlaps the period makes it ineligible, whatever its kind or actor. Eligibility takes no campaign, protocol, or shortlist input, so a new campaign or a revised protocol cannot restore an observed period.

Freezing encodes the candidate specification IDs and the preprocessing, training, portfolio-rule, and evaluation references as canonical JSON. The SHA-256 of those bytes is the shortlist identity. A confirmation run recomputes that identity and refuses a changed shortlist before it evaluates anything. An empty shortlist returns "no confirmation needed" without an eligibility check or an evaluation. Otherwise the run needs an eligible period and a recordable actor and time. The caller's evaluation receives the shortlist and that period, and it must return one outcome per candidate. The period is an optional run input rather than a frozen item, because an empty shortlist needs none and the eligibility check owns it.

The run returns a separate restricted report type. It is neither a package record nor artifact bytes, so `save_package` refuses it as the record, as artifact content, and as the confirmation field. Its text representation omits the outcomes. The report also carries an ordinary result exposure for the confirmed period, with the shortlist identity as its material digest. Adding that exposure to the lineage history makes the period ineligible, so informed follow-up needs a revised protocol and another eligible period.

Exposure history is caller-declared, as in [D006](#d006-keep-development-packages-self-contained). An omitted exposure makes a period look eligible. An evaluation that raises or returns the wrong candidates leaves no exposure, so the caller must record that observation. The caller is trusted to supply an evaluation that implements the frozen evaluation digest, because the module cannot bind Python code to it. Periods match by exact dataset ID, so callers must give related vintages one ID until a data policy defines related datasets. The type boundary stops accidental inclusion in ordinary exports, not deliberate copying of outcomes into a development trial. Statistical evaluation, restriction labels, walk-forward splits, and storage for restricted reports remain outside this module.

## D022. Index code from Git objects at a pinned commit

Selected for Phase 3 code understanding on 22 October 2026. The [code index](../src/quant_research/code_index.py) reads one commit's tree and blobs with `git ls-tree` and `git cat-file` and parses Python with `ast`. It never checks out files or reads the working tree or staging area, so uncommitted edits cannot enter an index. It never imports or executes repository code. It treats as checkouts the supplied directory and every directory above it that holds `.git`, searching upward both from the path as given and from the path it resolves to. It then runs the `git` in the first `PATH` directory that holds one and lies outside every checkout, and it chooses that `git` once per index. An enclosing checkout, such as a home directory that holds `.git` for dotfiles, makes every `git` under it ineligible, so indexing there fails unless another `git` lies outside it. It compares directories by identity, not by name, so a case-insensitive volume or a macOS firmlink cannot hide a checkout under another spelling. A `PATH` entry that leads into a checkout is skipped, whether it is empty, `.`, relative, or absolute, so a `git` file or symbolic link there never runs. An entry is also skipped when it passes through a checkout as written, such as `<checkout>/bin` or `<checkout>/bin/..` where a committed `bin` links elsewhere. A `PATH` entry that cannot be read as written, such as a symbolic-link loop, an overlong name, or a missing directory before `..`, is passed over. Indexing fails when the supplied path is not a directory, no other `git` is on `PATH`, or the chosen `git` cannot start, such as a script whose interpreter is missing. Git runs without replacement objects, inherited `GIT_` settings, or lazy fetching, so a partial clone with a missing blob fails instead of fetching it. The supplied repository's own Git configuration still applies. The index runs only `rev-parse`, `ls-tree`, and `cat-file`, with `core.fsmonitor` turned off, because `rev-parse` reads the index for a revision that names an index entry, such as `:0:app.py`. Tests run the index from inside the checkout with these on `PATH`: that checkout, a symbolic-link loop, an overlong name, a missing directory before `..`, a committed link that leads out of the checkout, a path through `..` after that link, and a link from outside to the checkout's `bin`. They also index a subdirectory, a symbolic link that leads out of the checkout, a symbolic link into another repository, a symbolic link from outside into a subdirectory, and, on a case-insensitive volume, another spelling of the checkout, all below a directory whose `.git` cannot be read. They show that a `git` file or symbolic link in the checkout, configured aliases, filters, text conversion, `core.fsmonitor`, the `post-index-change`, `reference-transaction`, and `pre-auto-gc` hooks, pagers, credential helpers, `gpg.program`, and a promisor remote's `core.sshCommand` do not run, even for such a revision. They also show that an index run imports no Python module.

The index trusts the caller's environment, which includes the `git` it finds, the global and system Git configuration, and the Python import path that loaded the indexer.
