It keeps the global and system configuration on purpose. Git honors the operator's repository policy, such as `safe.bareRepository` and `safe.directory`, only in those scopes and on the command line. An index that ignored them would read a bare repository that the operator's `safe.bareRepository = explicit` refuses. It would also refuse a repository owned by another user that the operator lists in `safe.directory`. The cost is that a malformed host configuration makes every index fail, and a host `trace2` target receives the index's Git events. The code index tests set a private `HOME` and `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, so the host's global configuration cannot change their results. The host's system configuration still applies to them. The index drops every inherited `GIT_` setting, so a test cannot pass it `GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM`. A `git` file can still run when `PATH` leads to it in a Git directory that a `.git` file names elsewhere, a working tree that `core.worktree` names elsewhere, a bare repository above a supplied subdirectory, or the main checkout of an indexed linked worktree, because none of them is a checkout around the supplied directory.

An index is a generation of one pinned input. It records the commit, its root tree, the indexer version `code-index/v1`, and the Python implementation and version that parsed it. Canonical JSON makes the output byte-identical for the same commit, indexer version, and interpreter. A different interpreter release can change `ast` positions or error text, and that output is a new generation, not a defect. `PYTHONINTMAXSTRDIGITS` and `pyproject.toml` nesting near Python's recursion limit can also change the bytes. This follows the layered identity in [section 5 of the SurrealDB plan](surrealdb-plan.md#5-ingestion-identity-and-publication).

Flat tables hold each fact once. Modules cover every regular tracked `.py` file, with its blob ID and an import name derived from `__init__.py` directories, as pytest's default import mode derives it. Symbols are classes and functions with the qualified names CPython gives them and line spans that start at the first decorator. A definition that its enclosing scope declares `global` has a top-level name. Imports link each importing file to the repository file that provides the module, or record the module as outside the repository. When several files share a module name, the importer's own import root wins, then the other roots in sorted order. Within one root, a package directory wins over a same-named module, as it does in Python. Test references come from files named `test_*.py`, `*_test.py`, or `conftest.py`, and follow re-exports, submodules, and class members to the deepest indexed symbol. Dependencies come from tracked `pyproject.toml` and `requirements*.txt` files. Requirement files join backslash continuations as pip does, so a comment line never continues onto the next requirement. A file that fails to parse becomes a failure record, stays in the module table, and contributes nothing else.

Resolution is static and approximate. A test file's imports bind names for the whole file, so a local name that shadows an import still counts as a reference. A name that a package binds shadows its same-named submodule. An `import` statement in the test file that names the submodule replaces that binding, because Python binds a submodule on its package when it first loads it. A binding imported from the submodule itself, as in `from .train import train`, stays, because the package loaded the submodule first. A package that loads the submodule another way, such as through a different module, is treated as not having loaded it. Star imports, `__all__`, dynamic imports, and attributes of instances are not followed. `setup.py` and Poetry tables are not read. Symbolic links, submodules, and non-UTF-8 paths are not supported. Nothing consumes the index yet, and real reference code arrives with the Phase 3 pilot.

## D023. Render research reports only from verified packages

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the initial research package export in [Phase 2](master-plan.md#phase-2-deliver-a-durable-research-workspace). `render_report` takes the `VerifiedPackage` that `open_package` returns and produces Markdown. It never reads package files itself, so an ordinary change to the manifest or an artifact fails verification before the report exists. A coordinated rewrite of content and checksums still verifies, as [D006](#d006-keep-development-packages-self-contained) records. The CLI opens, renders, and only then writes, so a failed open creates no output file and leaves an existing one unchanged. An output path inside the package directory is rejected, because an extra root entry would fail verification and a manifest path would overwrite evidence. The check compares the identity of the output path and its parent directories with the package directory, so a symlink or a differently cased name on a case-insensitive disk is rejected too. The output must name a regular file or a path that does not exist yet. A path ending in a separator, `/.`, or `/..`, an existing directory, and an existing FIFO or device such as `/dev/null` are rejected, and standard output serves streams instead. The operating system, not the path text, decides where the output is. Its directory must exist when the system walks it, so `missing/../r.md` and `file.md/../r.md` fail as they do in a shell instead of writing `r.md`, and a dangling symlink is refused. The CLI writes the report to a new file in the output's directory and renames it onto the output path, so a hard link to a package file placed elsewhere becomes a separate file instead of overwriting the package through the shared inode. A symlink to an existing file is followed, so the link stays and its target is replaced. Writing needs permission to create a file in the output's directory and to replace an existing output. A failed write leaves the output unchanged, and a replaced output, even a read-only one, takes a new file's default permissions.

The report reads only the manifest digest, the record, and the artifact records, and, since [D055](#d055-run-the-reference-experiment-through-one-audited-pipeline-against-a-frozen-experiment-file), the hash-verified bytes of each pilot evaluation artifact. It omits the package path, the render time, and the renderer's environment, so a copied or renamed package renders the same bytes. Trials, attempts, and exposures appear in their supplied order. The status summary follows the order of the `Status` type and includes zero counts. A package that selects no trial says so in place of the selected trial and its question, per [D060](#d060-let-a-package-record-no-selected-trial).

Every attempt appears with its status, detail, and limitations, including rejected, failed, null, and inconclusive ones. The report names no final or winning attempt, because a package carries no acceptance event. It states the package's disclosure coverage, that confirmation is unavailable, and that it makes no claim about investment usefulness. A no-improvement finding is a recorded research result, as [the product description](master-plan.md#the-product-we-are-building) requires.

Package text is arbitrary. The renderer joins its line breaks and escapes inline Markdown characters, so a detail stays on its own bullet line and cannot add a heading or another bullet. Control and format characters, such as terminal escapes and direction overrides, render as visible Python escapes, so a value cannot move the cursor or reorder text on a terminal. A long value can still wrap onto a new terminal row, but the Markdown keeps it in one bullet. The manifest remains the authority for exact text. The report lists artifact names, roles, sizes, and digests, but does not embed artifact bytes. Markdown is the only format. HTML, templates, comparisons across packages, and restricted confirmation reports are outside this increment.

## D024. Catalog datasets as a log of evidenced transitions

Selected for the pilot data catalog on 22 October 2026 after two design sketches converged on an append-only log and a second-opinion review. The [catalog module](../src/quant_research/catalog.py) records four transitions: documented, validated, approved, and revoked. A dataset's status is its latest transition, and one table lists the statuses each transition can follow. Every catalog value replays its log through that table, including a value parsed from bytes or built directly. A catalog therefore cannot hold an approval that its recorded evidence does not support.

Documentation records the owner, fields, plain access labels, known gaps, and vintage evidence for publication, revision, and arrival times. Evidence is a caller-supplied reference to material that someone can produce and recheck. It holds the material's SHA-256 digest and a summary. The catalog stores the reference and does not inspect the material. It cannot establish that the material exists, supports the claim, or carries vintage information in the underlying data rather than database load timestamps. The person who records the evidence owns that judgment. Each aspect without evidence appears as a gap and blocks approval. Known gaps are free text and do not block.

Validation records manual validation evidence. Approval must follow validation, requires evidence for every vintage aspect, and names a timing-and-access adapter. The catalog stores that reference and does not run or verify an adapter, and no adapter exists yet. Revocation can follow validation or approval, and history keeps the revoked events. A revoked dataset exposes no adapter. Documentation can change only while a dataset is documented or revoked, and validation must follow documentation. After a revocation, documentation is recorded again before any new validation. Each validation therefore applies to the documentation in force, and restoring a revoked dataset takes new documentation and validation events. A dataset's event cannot be dated before its latest event, so a validation cannot predate the documentation it attests. Events of different datasets are not ordered against each other.

The saved form is one canonical JSON document with a schema version and the ordered transitions, like a package manifest. JSON Lines would let a file truncated at a line boundary parse as an older valid catalog. The catalog imports the canonical JSON, digest, and UTC timestamp helpers of [records](../src/quant_research/records.py) instead of copying them a third time, as [D033](#d033-share-record-helpers-through-a-public-records-module) records. The SHA-256 of the saved bytes identifies a revision. Neither the bytes nor the digest prevent someone from rewriting history and recomputing the digest. Search matches casefolded terms against the name, owner, and fields, filters by status, and orders results by name. Separate writers append to separate copies, and parsing validates any merged log again.

Access constraints stay plain labels until a restriction model exists. Test entries are synthetic. No real dataset is cataloged or approved, and real entries wait for the Phase 0 inventory.

## D025. Carry restrictions with material and authority with the run

Selected on 22 October 2026. [quant_research.access](../src/quant_research/access.py) gives every later read and send path one rule set. Material carries `Restrictions`: restriction labels, the providers it is approved for, and its availability time. A run carries a `RunContext`: the labels it is cleared to read, its cutoff, its corpus generation, its permitted providers, and its memory pin.

Derived material takes the union of its inputs' labels, the intersection of their provider approvals, and the latest availability. The most restrictive input wins, and order and grouping do not change the result. Derivation time does not enter availability. The replay mode decides how a later derivation applies. Every construction states its labels, and approval is a positive grant, so material without one cannot be sent anywhere. A provider-to-label matrix on the context was rejected because material that ingestion failed to label would then reach every permitted provider.

Reading requires every label to be cleared and availability at or before the cutoff. Restrictions and contexts store their times in UTC, because datetimes that share a time zone compare by wall clock and misorder the repeated daylight-saving hour. Sending also requires a provider that both the run permits and the material is approved for. Checks return every applicable denial as a reason code. They carry no detail, because label names and future availability times would leak to a model that sees a denial.

Direct construction and dataclass replacement of a context raise, and every context refuses confirmation clearance and a naive cutoff. `issue_context` refuses a generation, clearance, provider, or memory pin that is not text. `issue_context` is the one trusted constructor. Only convention and review keep model-supplied values out of it, so integration PRs must audit its callers. A model-supplied bound reaches a context through `narrow`, which accepts an earlier cutoff and refuses a later one. A later cutoff, an extra provider, or different clearances require a new trusted call and produce a distinct context. The guard does not stop deliberate in-process forgery.

The memory pin is the head of the review-log prefix whose lessons the run may reuse ([D019](#d019-admit-lessons-only-through-recorded-review-events)). `issue_context` defaults it to `NO_MEMORY`, the head of the empty log, so a run that names no memory admits none. The identity includes the pin only when it is not `NO_MEMORY`, so a memoryless context keeps the identity that earlier records name. `narrow` keeps the pin, and another pin requires a new trusted call. The pin selects a log point, not a time, so review times stay apart from the cutoff.

Confirmation material is therefore denied to every context. Authorized review of the restricted confirmation report needs its own path, which [D048](#d048-review-restricted-confirmation-reports-through-audited-grants) adds. The context holds the corpus generation for retrieval to pin. Items are not checked against it, because the source-generation work defines generation identity.

The labels, providers, and grants in tests are test policies. Phase 0 decides which provider may receive which material. Source reads adopt the read check in [D036](#d036-filter-source-reads-through-the-trusted-run-context). Retrieval ([D040](#d040-retrieve-exactly-over-a-pinned-eligible-corpus)), provider calls ([D041](#d041-check-outbound-provider-content-against-the-run-and-a-versioned-provider-policy)), and agent tools ([D049](#d049-expose-evidence-to-agents-through-typed-tools-bound-to-the-run-context)) adopt these checks.

## D026. Qualify Temporal for durable campaign execution

Qualified on 22 October 2026 for Phase 2 of the [master plan](master-plan.md#phase-2-deliver-a-durable-research-workspace). The pinned versions are the `temporalio` Python SDK 1.34.0 in the lock and Temporal CLI 1.9.1 (Server 1.32.0, UI 2.54.1) from the Homebrew bottle or the [v1.9.1 release](https://github.com/temporalio/cli/releases/tag/v1.9.1). [The probe tests](../tests/test_execution.py) start `temporal server start-dev` on a free local port with in-memory storage. They fail with the install command when the CLI is missing, and they fail on any other CLI version until the probes run again and this entry changes. The probe module was not a campaign runner, and [D042](#d042-run-each-campaign-as-one-temporal-workflow-that-owns-its-logs) replaced it with one.

Each observation below was a passing probe against the real dev server. The runner tests of [D042](#d042-run-each-campaign-as-one-temporal-workflow-that-owns-its-logs) replaced the probes except the server-retry probe:

- After a worker receives SIGKILL mid-activity, the 2-second heartbeat timeout ends that attempt. A new worker replays the history, runs a second attempt, and the workflow completes once with its original run ID. In history, the heartbeat timeout takes 2 seconds, and the next workflow task then waits 10 seconds on the killed worker's sticky queue, the SDK default `sticky_queue_schedule_to_start_timeout`, before the 4-second job runs again. Two local runs showed this split, and no probe asserts it.
- A server-side retry policy leaves one `ActivityTaskStarted` event with the final attempt number and only the previous attempt's failure. Earlier failures are absent, and no workflow task runs between the attempts, so workflow code cannot count them against a budget. The probe workflow therefore disables server retries and schedules each attempt as its own activity with the ID `<job>/<n>`. History keeps every attempt's schedule and outcome.
- `start_campaign` uses the `USE_EXISTING` conflict policy and the `REJECT_DUPLICATE` reuse policy. A repeated start of a running campaign returns the same run, and a start after the campaign closes is rejected. Temporal's default policies reject the running duplicate but start a second run after close, which allows two results for one logical execution.
- Pause, annotate, and resume signals arrive in history in order. While the campaign is paused, it schedules no activity, and a query reports the paused state and the note.
- Cancellation with `WAIT_CANCELLATION_COMPLETED` stops the running activity. History records `ActivityTaskCanceled`, which shows that the activity itself stopped, and the workflow ends canceled.
- The workflow charges each attempt's declared cost before it schedules the attempt, failed attempts included. It returns `budget_exhausted` instead of starting an attempt that would exceed the limit.
- An external scheduler completes an attempt by workflow ID and activity ID through asynchronous completion. A second report for the same attempt and a late report for a superseded attempt both fail with `NOT_FOUND`, so the workflow accepts only the first report. The scheduler reports only the output, and the workflow takes the job and attempt from the activity it scheduled.

These probes do not close the Phase 2 exit gate. Costs are declared per attempt, not measured, and cancellation stops a local activity, not a cluster job. Both need the compute path that [Q005](open-questions.md#q005-which-infrastructure-and-provider-assumptions-are-correct) leaves open. The attempt ID can serve as the scheduler's idempotency key, but no probe shows that a real scheduler rejects a second submission after an ambiguous timeout. The 2-second heartbeat timeout leaves about 0.4 seconds of slack under the SDK's heartbeat throttle of 80 percent of the timeout. This figure is inferred and has not been measured under load. Temporal applies `REJECT_DUPLICATE` only to closed executions that the namespace still retains, per [its workflow ID documentation](https://docs.temporal.io/workflow-execution/workflowid-runid). After retention removes a closed campaign, the policy no longer covers its ID, so the research record must keep campaign IDs single-use. Production persistence, upgrades, backup, and visibility search are unqualified, and Temporal still needs a [supported persistence backend](surrealdb-plan.md#1-responsibilities-and-authority). The D042 runner record links workflow, run, and attempt IDs. The manual hosted workflow installs the CLI from the v1.9.1 Linux archive after checking its pinned SHA-256. GitHub refused the first hosted run on 22 October 2026 for an account billing limit, so no hosted run has passed yet. The same install script and probes passed in an Ubuntu 24.04 amd64 container.

## D027. Qualify the pinned SurrealDB server and client over WebSocket

Selected on 22 October 2026 for increment 1 of the [SurrealDB plan](surrealdb-plan.md#9-delivery-increments). Pin the server at 3.3.4 and the Python SDK at `surrealdb==2.0.0`, the plan's candidates and the newest stable releases available on that date. Install the server with `brew install surrealdb/tap/surreal-db`. The tap's `surreal` formula now installs a launcher that downloads components itself; `surreal-db` installs the database binary from the same archive and checksum as the GitHub release. The tap has no versioned formula, so once it moves past 3.3.4 the version probe fails and the release archive becomes the install route. [The store module](../src/quant_research/store.py) holds the local configuration: a loopback RocksDB server with a generated root password, a WebSocket connection, and a checked query. RocksDB keeps the first root user, so a server reopened on existing storage needs that first password. [The probe suite](../tests/test_store.py) starts that server with temporary storage and asserts the versions, the checked query, and the semantics listed below. A missing `surreal-db` fails the suite with the install command.

Use WebSocket, because the SDK's HTTP client rejected client-side transactions in a scratch probe. Use `store.query` rather than the SDK's `query`. The SDK returns only the first statement's result and ignores errors in later statements, so `RETURN 1; THROW 'boom'` returned 1. `store.query` raises every failed statement together, so a failed transaction reports its cause beside the statements it prevented. The current documentation describes a query builder with one result per statement; the installed 2.0.0 does not have it. Selecting an undefined table raises an error rather than returning no rows. A request without BEGIN and COMMIT is not atomic, so statements before a failure stay written.

The registry must design around these observed semantics:

- **Identity.** `RecordID("trial", uuid)` and `type::record('trial', $u)` address one UUID-keyed record. A record keyed by the same UUID text is a different record, so pass UUID objects.
- **Time.** The server keeps nanoseconds, but the SDK truncates them to microseconds. Offsets become UTC, and a naive datetime is read as UTC without an error. Reject naive input at the registry boundary, and keep source timestamps as text where precision or offset matters.
- **Optional values.** Python `None` writes NONE, which removes the field. An `option<>` field rejects NULL, and a stored NULL reads back as `None`, so a read and rewrite can turn NULL into an absent field.
- **Decimals.** A `Decimal("1.2300")` parameter returns `1.23`. The typed path keeps the value but not the scale, so store package decimal text as strings.
- **Transactions.** Snapshot isolation detects conflicts at commit. Two transactions writing one record fail the second commit with a retryable `TransactionConflict`. Predicates are unprotected. Two transactions that each count zero rows and insert both commit. Express a single-winner decision, such as result acceptance, as a guard record or unique index.
- **Uniqueness.** A sequential duplicate on a UNIQUE index raises `InternalError` naming the index, not `AlreadyExistsError`. A concurrent duplicate fails first as `TransactionConflict`. Idempotent import must treat both as duplicate outcomes and recheck after a retry. An existing record ID raises `AlreadyExistsError`. A schema coercion failure, such as NULL in an `option<>` field, is also `InternalError`, so the kind alone cannot separate it from a duplicate.
- **Backup and restore.** CLI export and import into a fresh server preserved records, UUID keys, relation IDs and endpoints, traversals, nanosecond timestamps, decimal strings, and unique-index enforcement. Exporting a misspelled database exits successfully with no records, so a backup must be checked for its contents.
- **Filtered vector search.** A scope-filtered HNSW query matched an exact Python baseline when 20 excluded vectors were nearer than every eligible one, even with a candidate list of 5, so the filter applies during the search rather than after it. With k above the eligible count it returned only eligible records, including duplicate vectors. `EXPLAIN` showed the index scan. Without the HNSW index the same query returns no rows rather than an error, so retrieval must confirm the index exists. Index distances match exact float64 distances within the asserted relative tolerance of 1e-6; a scratch probe saw differences near 3e-8.

These probes do not qualify permissions, which the plan's qualification gate names, or record-level access and non-root authentication. [D038](#d038-restrict-reads-through-record-access-and-make-denied-writes-throw) probes them and corrects the backup result for databases with record access. Increment 1 also lists migrations; none exist, because the registry has no schema yet. Vector recall covers a 27-row fixture, not a corpus, a compaction period, or the permission-filter fix the plan asks to pin. Durability after a crash and any hosted backend remain untested. The SDK triggers a deprecation warning from the locked `websockets` 17.3, so a `websockets` upgrade needs its own check. The manual hosted workflow installs `surreal-db` from the 3.3.4 Linux release archive after checking its pinned SHA-256. No hosted run has passed yet, but the same install script and probes passed in an Ubuntu 24.04 amd64 container.

## D028. Keep replay descriptive and record evaluation parameters with each result

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the follow-up to [D015](#d015-evaluate-pilot-signals-against-their-recorded-trials). Replay does not adopt the pilot evaluation profile. `evaluate` needs at least 30 equally spaced, non-overlapping period returns. The version 1 replay contract yields signal and label pairs, and every committed fixture, including the one the wheel consumer writes, places all its decisions at one time. A per-period series from any of them has one value. Labels have the unit `synthetic_target`, not a return. Weighting labels by their signals would turn them into a return only through a position rule. [D010](#d010-replay-one-declared-synthetic-causal-profile) and [the replay plan](initial-prs.md#pr-3-replay-a-causal-development-fixture) exclude portfolio construction and statistical significance and alpha claims from replay. The contract also declares no label horizon, so replay cannot show that consecutive periods do not overlap. An adopted evaluation would return `too_few_observations` for every fixture and add a code path with no other result.

Replay keeps the `descriptive-pearson/v1` diagnostic and its outcome mapping. The contract requires that identifier, so a new one would reject every existing fixture. The first adopter needs a declared series of at least 30 period returns, such as the Phase 1 reference experiment's. It maps `Invalid("zero_variance")` to an inconclusive package outcome, as D015 requires.

Each evaluation result now records the parameters that produced it. `evaluate` builds a frozen `Profile` of the minimum effect, the subperiod count, and alpha, and `Profile` rejects an invalid declaration. A boolean, a value that is neither an `int` nor a `float`, and a subperiod count that is not an `int` are invalid. `Profile` stores the minimum effect and alpha as floats, so declaring 1 and 1.0 saves the same bytes. It also records the procedure version, `pilot-evaluation/v1`, which a caller cannot set. A change that can alter any result for the same inputs and parameters, such as to the verdict rules, the intervals, the minimum length, the subperiod bounds, or the recorded form, takes a new version. A result from the changed procedure then differs from older results in its recorded version, even where its numbers agree. The version uses the form of a specification's `evaluation_profile` field, so the first adopter can write it there. `Evaluation` and `Invalid` both carry it, because the subperiod count also sets the minimum length and the subperiods that the zero-variance check reads. An adopter saves the result as an artifact, for example its `asdict` form as JSON, and the package then shows which parameters produced the verdict. We rejected new `Specification` fields, because they would change every `spec_id` and the hashes of the committed example package. We also rejected, for now, a parameter string in the specification's existing `evaluation_profile` field, because it needs a number format and a parse rule that nothing reads yet.

A saved result shows which procedure version and parameters produced a verdict. It does not show that they were declared before the outcomes were seen. Binding them to a trial's identity needs them in its specification, and the first adopter writes that specification.

## D029. Treat the latest membership vintage as complete

Selected for causal replay on 22 October 2026 after fixture probes compared two rules. [D010](#d010-replay-one-declared-synthetic-causal-profile) originally selected the latest known membership record whose session interval covers the decision's session. Under that rule, a later record could add sessions or exclude them but could not shorten an interval. Suppose entity A is known on 4 January as a member for s0 through s2 and on 7 January as a member for s0 only. A decision at s1 after 7 January still found the 4 January record and produced a signal.

Replay now groups an entity's records by `known_at`, and each group is a vintage. The vintage at a decision is the entity's latest `known_at` at or before the decision, whichever sessions its records cover. That vintage is the entity's whole known membership, so a session that none of its records covers is not a member session. Records can share a `known_at` when their intervals do not overlap, so one vintage can list s0 and s2 and leave out s1. `included: false` still marks a session as known excluded. It is also the only way to remove every session, because an entity without records has no vintage.

Selection provenance gives one of four reasons when the entity is not a member at the decision's session. `membership_excluded` means the vintage covers the session with `included: false`. `membership_superseded` means an older known vintage covered the session and the current vintage does not. `membership_unavailable` means only records known after the decision cover the session. `membership_missing` means no record covers the session. The superseded reason is new, because a dropped entity reported as missing would look as if no source had ever listed it. Every selection records its `vintage`, the `known_at` of the entity's vintage at the decision, or null when no record of the entity was known by then. A superseded decision records no membership record, because no record in the current vintage covers its session, so its `vintage` names that current vintage. A vintage known after the decision that covers the session again does not change the reason.

Each vintage must restate every session where the entity stays a member. A source that sends only corrections needs merging into full vintages before replay. The two rules fail in opposite directions. The interval rule keeps a stale member and cites a record that looks valid. The vintage rule drops the sessions a partial vintage leaves out and reports them as superseded, so provenance shows the failure. No visible fixture has a decision that the change affects, so their outputs do not change. [Architecture](architecture.md#replay-the-synthetic-development-fixture) notes the one known-answer vintage that leaves out a session. The rule applies per entity, so a vintage of other entities does not remove an entity it leaves out.

## D030. Accept results through explicit events

Selected on 22 October 2026 for [Phase 2 of the master plan](master-plan.md#phase-2-deliver-a-durable-research-workspace), which makes result acceptance an explicit event and never the latest or successful attempt by default. [quant_research.acceptance](../src/quant_research/acceptance.py) keeps an append-only list of three events. `Submitted` records an attempt in a run, `Completed` records its outcome, and `Accepted` names the attempt whose outcome is the run's result. Every reading uses a fold of that list, as the [resource ledger](#d016-account-for-resources-in-an-append-only-ledger) does. The log applies each event to its fold as it records the event, so a check never re-reads earlier events.

A logical execution is one package run. Retries share a run ID and name a predecessor, so they compete for the run's single acceptance. A repeat has a new run ID and gets its own acceptance. The events use the trial, run, and attempt UUIDs of [portable packages](../src/quant_research/packages.py), and a completion carries the package `Outcome`.

Nothing is accepted by default. A run with a successful attempt reads as unaccepted until an `Accepted` event names an attempt with an actor and a reason. Any completed attempt can be accepted, including a null, inconclusive, rejected, or failed one, because acceptance chooses the result that later work cites and does not claim success. An attempt without a recorded outcome cannot be accepted, so an interrupted execution stays visible and unaccepted.

Acceptance is a compare-and-swap. Its `supersedes` field must name the run's currently accepted attempt, or be empty when the run has none. The log therefore refuses a second plain acceptance and a supersession that names a formerly accepted attempt. A supersession carries its own actor and reason, and the run's decision history keeps every acceptance. A supersession may name the accepted attempt itself, which records a new reason without changing the result. A separate withdrawal event would leave the run unaccepted between two events, so one event replaces the accepted attempt.

An event identical to one already recorded appends nothing, so a redelivered submission, completion, or acceptance changes nothing. A conflicting event raises `AcceptanceError` with the reason. A second completion of the same attempt conflicts when its outcome or `finished_at` differs. `finished_at` is the attempt's own finish time, not the time the event arrived, so a redelivery repeats it exactly.

The log is deliberately looser than the package format. A retry may start while its predecessor has no outcome, which is the ambiguous submission that the Phase 2 exit gate names. A late completion of the first attempt still records after its retry is accepted. The log checks neither time order nor result artifacts.

The canonical encoding is the package's canonical JSON with a schema version and the events in record order. Loading replays every event through the same checks and requires the replay to reproduce the input bytes, so a stored duplicate or a refused sequence fails to load. `record` validates an in-memory event by parsing its own encoding, so both paths apply one set of checks.

These limits are deliberate:

- The log is in memory with one writer. The registry must persist it and apply each event as one atomic check and append per run before concurrent workers record events.
- A package rejects a retry that starts before its predecessor finishes, so it cannot yet hold both attempts of an ambiguous submission. Exporting such a run needs a package format change.
- Events record facts and decisions but not when the log received them.

## D031. Record campaign steering in an append-only log

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the Phase 2 steering increment, on top of [campaign specifications](#d013-version-campaign-specifications-as-content-addressed-revisions). [quant_research.steering](../src/quant_research/steering.py) keeps one append-only event log per campaign. An event pauses, resumes, cancels, annotates, or steers the campaign, and each event names an actor, a time, and a reason. The control state is a fold over the log.

A campaign starts running. A pause moves it to paused, a resume returns it to running, and a cancel from either state ends it. Cancelled is final. The log refuses a pause, resume, or steer after a cancel and names the current state. A command that names the current state, such as a second cancel, is recorded and changes nothing, so a repeated cancel is idempotent. An event identical to a recorded one appends nothing, so an exact retry leaves the log unchanged, as in [D016](#d016-account-for-resources-in-an-append-only-ledger). Annotations never change the state, and the log accepts them after a cancel. Event times must not decrease.

A steer records a methodology or scope change as a decision. It names a parent revision and the child revision that `campaigns.revise` created from it, on the same branch or a new one. The child must change at least one specification section. The log refuses a steer without such a revision, and a note that changes nothing is an annotation. The steer takes its actor and reason from the child revision, so the decision and the revision cannot disagree about who decided or why. Its time is when the log records the decision, which can be later than the revision's creation. That time must not precede the revision's creation or the log's last event. So a revision made before a pause can be steered after it, and steers to two branches can be recorded in either order. The log steers to a revision at most once. Under D013 the child carries its parent's trials and exposures, and the parent stays in the campaign history, so earlier results stay attached and visible. A branch leaves its parent's line in place, and both lines can be steered again. The control state covers the whole campaign. No branch has a state of its own.

The log serializes to canonical JSON. Reading a log requires the opened campaign, and every steer must match the actor, reason, and parent of a revision that the campaign holds and must not precede that revision's creation, so save the child revision before the log that names it. Three alternatives were rejected. Refusing a repeated cancel would turn a retried cancel into an error. Tracking one current revision in the log would stop the older line of a branch from being steered. Dating a steer at its revision's creation would refuse a steer recorded after a later event, such as a pause.

The log has no file store or lock, and the [D042](#d042-run-each-campaign-as-one-temporal-workflow-that-owns-its-logs) runner enforces it. A revision made directly through `revise` needs no steer, so the record of methodology changes is complete only when researchers steer through this module. The registry must persist the log before Phase 4 relies on it for "Scope or methodology changes become recorded decisions."

## D032. Register frozen procedures before observing them prospectively

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the prospective observation steps in Phases 4 and 7 of the [master plan](master-plan.md). [quant_research.prospective](../src/quant_research/prospective.py) keeps one append-only log of registrations and observations. A procedure is a [frozen shortlist](#d021-gate-final-confirmation-on-exposure-history-and-frozen-shortlists), which may be empty, together with the [model records](#d012-record-model-provenance-and-role-versions-as-inactive-data) available for it, unknown versions and cutoffs included. Its identity is the SHA-256 of canonical JSON that holds the digest of the shortlist's frozen contents and the model records in their supplied order, so a shortlist changed after freezing yields a different identity. A registration binds that procedure to a registration time. It refuses a shortlist that changed after freezing and a repeated model record.

An observation names its registration, the procedure identity that produced it, a data window, the earliest time any data it read became available, its observation time, and an outcome. The window names the period the data describes and is not compared with the registration time, because data about an earlier period can first become available after registration. The log refuses an observation whose data was available at or before the registration time, whose procedure identity differs from its registration's, or whose observation time precedes its data. A changed procedure therefore needs its own registration, and the earlier registration and its observations stay in the log. Registering an unchanged procedure again adds a second registration with a later, stricter boundary. Event times never decrease, so a registration cannot be dated behind events already recorded. Appending an event that is already in the log returns the same log, and a stored log that repeats an event is refused. A retry with a different outcome on the same window is a different observation, so the log keeps both. The report lists each registration with its observations and their windows in log order.

The stored form is one canonical JSON document with a schema version, like a [campaign revision](#d013-version-campaign-specifications-as-content-addressed-revisions). Loading refuses non-canonical bytes, rebuilds each shortlist with `freeze` to check its identity, and replays every event through the same rules. Appending parses each event the same way, so any log the registry accepts dumps and loads to the same bytes.

Availability times are caller-declared, like the exposure history in [D006](#d006-keep-development-packages-self-contained). A caller who misstates when data arrived defeats the check. The stored bytes carry no signature, so a hand edit that leaves a consistent log, such as a dropped observation, loads cleanly. As in D021, the caller is trusted to run an evaluation that implements the frozen evaluation digest. The module runs no evaluation, schedules nothing, and has no store, so the caller keeps the bytes. [D068](#d068-register-campaign-procedures-before-later-released-outcomes) adds a local store and evidence-bearing campaign registrations while preserving these legacy records. Prospective conclusions need data that arrives after registration, and no engineering release can supply it early.

## D033. Share record helpers through a public records module

Selected on 22 October 2026 as a behavior-preserving refactor. Thirteen modules imported the private canonical JSON and record-parsing helpers of [packages](../src/quant_research/packages.py), because the feature PRs that added them left packages unchanged. [quant_research.records](../src/quant_research/records.py) now holds those helpers under public names. `canonical_bytes` writes canonical JSON, `json_default` and `format_time` encode UUIDs and times, and the `parse_` functions check fields, text, digests, names, UUIDs, times, and arrays. Every caller imports them from records, and the private names no longer exist. The exposure and outcome parsers build a package `ExposureEvent` and `Outcome`, so they stay in packages under the public names `parse_exposure` and `parse_outcome`. Replay, workflows, and reproduction keep private helpers with similar names, because those raise each module's own error type.

A second pass gave the other helpers that one module imported from another a public home. `records.unique_keys` is the duplicate-key hook that replay, reconcile, providers, agent tools, and the capability loaders pass to `json.loads`. It moved from replay and still raises a plain `ValueError`. Replay, reconcile, and the capability loaders convert it to their own errors, providers reports the reply as malformed output, and agent tools refuse the arguments as invalid. The others stay in their modules under public names, because they raise that module's error or encode its values. `access.frozen` refuses a string where a collection belongs. `campaigns.unique` refuses a repeated value, and the campaign runner writes, reads, and links revision files with `campaigns.file_bytes`, `parse_file`, and `check_link`. `model_provenance.json_default` encodes a date or `UNKNOWN`. `accounting.parse_usage` reads a stored usage, and `accounting.EXACT` is the ledger's decimal context, which providers also uses to write call logs. Sources shares ten store helpers with locators and retrieval. Most drop the underscore. `_extraction_id` becomes `identify_extraction`, because a property and a caller's local variable already use `extraction_id`, and `_step` becomes `parse_step` like the other parsers. Each body and message is unchanged, and the private names no longer exist. Capability once kept its own duplicate-key hook with a different message. Its loaders already wrapped that hook's error as `CapabilityError`, so the shared hook changes only the wrapped reason. Records also holds `canonical_json` and `write_once`, which the file stores share. `canonical_json` returns a JSON value only when its bytes are already canonical and raises `PackageError` otherwise. `write_once` creates a file exclusively and reports whether the stored bytes match.

A third pass moved the numbered-file loop of the resource ledger store, the capability exposure ledger, and the call log into records. `read_log` yields the canonical JSON value of each file from `0.json` in order. It skips hidden files, such as the temporary files an interrupted write leaves, and raises `PackageError` for a gap, a stray file, or noncanonical bytes. It reads each file only when the caller asks for the next value, so a store still reports the first fault in file order. `append_log` writes the bytes that the caller's attempt returns under the number that attempt replayed, with `write_once`. When another writer has already taken that number with other bytes, it runs the attempt again on the longer log. Each store keeps its own fold, its own error class, and its own messages, and passes the file labels the old loops used. A [test](../tests/test_records.py) pins every stored byte and every refusal of the three stores, and it passes unchanged against the code before the move. The hash-chained log of the [review store](#d048-review-restricted-confirmation-reports-through-audited-grants) later moved onto the same pair. It hashes the canonical bytes of each value that `read_log` yields, and these equal the stored bytes because `read_log` yields only canonical files. A missing log directory still reads as an empty log. An error for an entry that is noncanonical or not valid JSON now names its file, as in `Log entry 1.json`, because `read_log` builds that label. A [test](../tests/test_confirmation_review.py) pins the review store's bytes and refusals. It passed against the code before the move, and the move changed only those two labels in it.

The helpers still raise `PackageError`, and the class moved with them into records. Packages imports it to raise its own errors, and every other module and test imports it from records. It keeps its name, its `ValueError` base, its docstring, and every message, and all modules share one class object. Each `except PackageError` clause and each test that expects `PackageError` therefore matches the same failures as before. Sources, catalog, reproduction, and workflows still convert it to their own errors, and confirmation still raises it for an invalid actor or time. Only the qualified name in a traceback changes, from `quant_research.packages.PackageError` to `quant_research.records.PackageError`. The old import path still resolves because packages imports the class, but no module in this repository uses it.

Two alternatives were rejected. A new `RecordError` base with `PackageError` beneath it would let helper failures escape every `except PackageError` clause, including the CLI's and the tests', unless each module added a wrapper. An alias would change the class name that tracebacks print.

The move changes no encoding. `canonical_bytes` keeps the `json.dumps` options, the trailing newline, and the UTF-8 check, so saved packages, campaign revisions, source stores, and catalogs keep their bytes and digests.

## D034. Compare the selected trials of two verified packages

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the experiment comparisons in [Phase 2 of the master plan](master-plan.md#phase-2-deliver-a-durable-research-workspace). [quant_research.compare](../src/quant_research/compare.py) compares the selected trials of two packages. `compare_packages` accepts only `VerifiedPackage` values, so a caller must open both packages first. `render_comparison` turns the result into deterministic text, and `quant-research compare A B` prints that text. The command exits 1 with the verifier's reason when either package fails verification, and it prints nothing else. Different packages still exit 0. A package that selects no trial is refused with its side named, per [D060](#d060-let-a-package-record-no-selected-trial).

The comparison holds rows for the specification, the inputs, the versions, and the outcome, plus metrics and the lineage relation. The row sections split the selected trial's specification fields, so no field appears twice. Each side's package digest, trial, lineage, and specification ID are a header. They are not rows, because two different packages always differ in them. A row is marked same, different, only in A, or only in B. `differences` lists every row and metric that is not the same, so a package compared with itself has none.

Inputs pair by their slot in the specification. The data contract, the implementation, and the environment lock each have one slot, and the input digests pair by position. Two inputs are identical when their SHA-256 digests are equal. Artifact names and roles are not compared. Replay puts the attempt ID in every artifact name, and roles are free text that authors choose.

A package has no winning attempt. The outcome and metrics come from the last supplied attempt of each selected trial, which is the attempt that the replay receipt reports. The text states that rule, and the outcome rows include each trial's attempt count. A trial with no attempts, such as a planned trial, shows only its count of 0 and no metrics. Limitations pair by position, one row each, because a limitation can itself contain a semicolon. The evaluation profile defines the metrics. The only entry, `descriptive-pearson/v1`, reads the fields of that attempt's `diagnostic` result artifact. A package with another profile shows no metrics. Each metric difference is B minus A, computed exactly in `Decimal`. Verification checks artifact digests, not content, so a package written with `save_package` can declare the profile and carry a different diagnostic. When that diagnostic is not a JSON object whose metric fields are integers or finite decimal strings, `compare_packages` raises `CompareError`. The command then exits 1 with the reason and prints no comparison.

The lineage relation is "same lineage" when the selected trials share a lineage ID. Otherwise it is "linked" when the trials' ancestor sets intersect. An ancestor set is the selected trial plus every trial that its links reach in its own package. Otherwise the relation is "unrelated". The comparison also lists the trials that both ancestor sets share. Replay gives a new trial the lineage of its first supplied parent. A replay package is therefore "linked" to a parent, rather than in its lineage, only when that parent came from another lineage.

The text contains no filesystem paths, so copies of the same packages give identical bytes. Every text value passes through `single_line` from the [report renderer](../src/quant_research/report.py). It replaces line breaks with spaces and escapes control and format characters, so package text cannot add rows or send terminal control sequences.

These limits are deliberate:

- Only the selected trials are compared. Other supplied trials, other attempts, and exposure events appear only through the attempt count and the lineage relation.
- The comparison reports differences and does not rank outcomes or judge significance.
- Artifact bytes are compared only through their digests.
- Metric strings are parsed by `Decimal`, which accepts more spellings than the replay contract writes. A metric with an extreme exponent, such as `1E+999999999999`, can exhaust memory when its difference is computed exactly. Replay never writes such values.
- Two values that differ only in line breaks or in escaped characters print the same text, although their row is marked different. The manifest holds the exact values.

## D035. Reconcile packages against declared tolerances and an exact recomputation

Selected for the Phase 1 exit gate on 22 October 2026. [quant_research.reconcile](../src/quant_research/reconcile.py) checks the latest attempt of a package's selected trial in two ways. It compares the recorded diagnostic metrics with a reference file, and it compares them with a recomputation from the package's saved raw input. `quant-research reconcile package PACKAGE --reference FILE` prints one record and exits 1 on any disagreement. The `reconcile` function returns the same result for a notebook.

The reference file is version 1 JSON. Each metric declares a value and an absolute tolerance, so the tolerances exist before the comparison runs, and the record keeps the file's SHA-256 digest. A metric agrees when the absolute difference between the recorded and reference values is at most its tolerance. The command refuses a reference that omits a tolerance, repeats a key, uses a JSON float, declares a negative tolerance, contains text that UTF-8 cannot encode, such as a lone surrogate in a metric name, or writes a number with more than 1000 digits after its leading zeros or an exponent outside [-1000, 1000] in scientific notation. Within those bounds, the record can print every exact difference. A metric that the package records and the reference omits, or the reverse, is a disagreement. The record reports it instead of skipping it.

The recomputation reads the raw input artifact that the specification's input digests name. It implements the descriptive Pearson profile from the rules in [D010](#d010-replay-one-declared-synthetic-causal-profile) and the membership vintages in [D029](#d029-treat-the-latest-membership-vintage-as-complete). It does not call replay. It selects inputs, forms signals, joins labels, and computes the centered sums with exact rationals. It then applies the profile's declared roundings at the contract's decimal precision with half-even rounding. These cover the signal subtraction, the three centered sums, and the two coefficient steps. Because the roundings belong to the profile, each recomputed value must equal the recorded value exactly. A package that verifies but records a wrong value fails this check, even when the reference declares the same wrong value.

The record is canonical JSON, as in [packages](../src/quant_research/packages.py). It names the package digest, the attempt, and the reference digest. Each comparison lists every metric with its recorded value, expected value, tolerance, exact difference of the recorded value minus the expected value, and status. The record omits the package path, so a copied package produces the same bytes.

These limits are deliberate:

- Reconciliation supports only the version 1 descriptive Pearson profile and the latest attempt of the selected trial. It refuses a rejected attempt, which has no diagnostic, and a package that selects no trial, per [D060](#d060-let-a-package-record-no-selected-trial).
- The recomputation is separate code written from the same D010 and D029 rules. An error in those rules would appear in both results, and a change to them must change both.
- It compares metrics, not individual signals or selected versions.
- Tolerances are absolute. There is no relative tolerance.
- The [known-answer reference](../tests/fixtures/reconcile/known-answer.json) is a synthetic development fixture. Real baseline agreement comes from the series reconciliation that closed [Q003](open-questions.md#q003-what-independent-baseline-defines-agreement) in [D055](#d055-run-the-reference-experiment-through-one-audited-pipeline-against-a-frozen-experiment-file).

## D036. Filter source reads through the trusted run context

Selected on 22 October 2026. [quant_research.sources](../src/quant_research/sources.py) applies the [D025](#d025-carry-restrictions-with-material-and-authority-with-the-run) read check to published source generations.

Each source declaration states its restriction labels and provider approvals beside `source_available_at`. Together they form the occurrence's `Restrictions`. Both sets enter the occurrence ID, so identical bytes under different restrictions are two occurrences. If a stored record's labels change, the record no longer matches its ID, and reads raise. An empty label set means the material is unrestricted. An empty approval set means no provider may receive it.

`published_generations` takes a `RunContext` in place of a cutoff. It returns a generation only when the context clears every label on its occurrence and the occurrence's `source_available_at` is at or before the context's cutoff. It omits every other generation without a reason, because exclusion is a legitimate answer and a reason would leak labels or future availability. To read with an earlier cutoff, a caller narrows the context with `RunContext.narrow`.

Malformed policy input fails explicitly. An argument that is not a `RunContext` raises `SourceError`, so a substitute object cannot make every generation visible. A label or approval set given as a string, an empty name, and a stored value that is not text also raise `SourceError`. A set given as `None` or with mixed element types raises `TypeError`. The read verifies every published generation before it filters, so a damaged generation raises even for a context that may not read it. The error names generation and occurrence IDs. Neither it, the exceptions it chains, nor its formatted traceback contains chunk text. The chained traceback's frames still hold the bytes being read, so a handler that prints frame locals can show that text. Extractor output that is not exactly a `str`, or text that cannot encode as UTF-8, such as a lone surrogate from a custom extractor, raises `SourceError` that names the occurrence and chains nothing, for both `publish_generation` and locator publication. A `str` subclass is refused because it can override the methods that check, hash, and store the text. An invalid chunk span or location is named by its index and never quoted, and a location's kind must be exactly a `str`. The frames of these errors still hold the text. An exception that an extractor, chunker, or locator raises itself keeps any text it carries. An `OSError`, `PackageError`, or `UnicodeEncodeError` is wrapped in `SourceError` with its message, and any other exception propagates unchanged. A chunker or locator result that Python cannot iterate or unpack raises Python's own `TypeError` unwrapped, and its message names the result's type. A callable that names a type after the text therefore puts the text in that error. These rules keep the text out of the errors these modules write, not out of errors a callable causes.

These limits are deliberate:

- `access_domain` and `rights` remain descriptive text. Only the labels and `source_available_at` decide reads.
- The read does not check the context's corpus generation. Retrieval pins it, per [D040](#d040-retrieve-exactly-over-a-pinned-eligible-corpus).
- `publish_generation` belongs to ingestion. It returns the generation it wrote and takes no context.
- Each occurrence stores its provider approvals. The provider gateway's send check ([D041](#d041-check-outbound-provider-content-against-the-run-and-a-versioned-provider-policy)) requires a material's approval to name the provider, but no path yet turns source generations or retrieved units into provider material.

## D038. Restrict reads through record access and make denied writes throw

Selected on 22 October 2026 after probing what [D027](#d027-qualify-the-pinned-surrealdb-server-and-client-over-websocket) left untested. The server and SDK pins do not change. [The probe suite](../tests/test_store.py) adds ten tests against the local 3.3.4 server over WebSocket. They define table and field PERMISSIONS, a `DEFINE ACCESS` record method that checks an argon2 password, and namespace and database users.

Select permissions filter only record-access users. Serve restricted reads through a record-access session, and never through a system user. The registry must design around these observed semantics:

- **Record access.** Signin with the namespace, database, access method, and variables returns a token. The session's `$auth` is the account record and its `$access` is the method name. A wrong password and an unknown account both raise `NotFoundError` with the same message. An unknown method raises `InternalError`. A connection that has not signed in raises `NotAllowedError` on every query. A record user cannot run `INFO FOR DB` or define anything.
- **System users.** A database user signs in only with its namespace and database, and a namespace user signs in with its namespace. A database user cannot `USE` another database, and a namespace user cannot `USE` another namespace. VIEWER reads definitions with password hashes redacted and cannot define anything. EDITOR defines tables but not users. A namespace OWNER defines database users. A system user's `$auth` is NONE, and its reads ignore select permissions, so a VIEWER sees restricted rows.
- **VIEWER writes.** VIEWER is not read-only. The server checks its writes against table permissions with `$auth` set to NONE. A table with `FOR create NONE` or no PERMISSIONS clause returns no rows. A table with `FOR create FULL` accepts the write. So does `FOR create WHERE owner = $auth.id` for a record without an owner, because NONE equals NONE. EDITOR writes ignore table permissions. Write a permission so that it fails when `$auth` is NONE.
- **Reads.** A select permission removes restricted rows from table scans, direct ID selects, and `count`. `record::exists` returns false for them. A direct select of a restricted ID returns no rows rather than an error. A field permission removes the field from the returned record. A permission clause reads the account through `$auth` even when the account table is `PERMISSIONS NONE`. A user's own statement reads it under the user's permissions, so there `$auth.id` is NONE and only bare `$auth` names the account.
- **Graph traversal.** `->edge->table` and `<-edge<-table` return only endpoints the user may select. A relation table without a PERMISSIONS clause hides every edge, so traversals return nothing, even between public records. A relation table with `FOR select FULL` returns restricted endpoint IDs in its `in` and `out` fields. A record link field also returns a restricted ID, and dereferencing the link returns NONE. An edge select permission that checks both endpoints hides those edges. Give every relation table that check, and keep meaning out of record IDs.
- **Vector search.** A record user's HNSW query with no scope filter returned the nearest eligible records. It did so with a candidate list of 5 and 20 nearer restricted vectors, so the permission applies during the search, like the explicit filter in D027.
- **Denied writes.** A CREATE, INSERT, UPDATE, UPSERT, or DELETE that a WHERE clause or NONE denies returns no rows, raises nothing, and leaves the record unchanged. A permission that throws on denial raises `ThrownError` and still allows a permitted write. The probe uses `FOR create WHERE IF writer = $auth { true } ELSE { THROW 'create denied' }`. A field `ASSERT $value = $auth` raises `InternalError`. Make every registry write permission throw. Checking for a returned record catches a denied CREATE, INSERT, UPDATE, or UPSERT, but a scratch probe showed that a plain DELETE returns no rows even when it succeeds.
- **Error messages.** A record user's write that collides with a restricted record reveals it. A duplicate value on a UNIQUE index names the restricted record and the value. Reusing a restricted record's ID raises `AlreadyExistsError` with that ID. Unique indexes and caller-chosen IDs therefore let writers to a table test for restricted rows.
- **Export.** The server refuses a record user's export with HTTP 403. Any system user, VIEWER included, exports every record whatever the select permissions. The export also carries every database user's password hash unredacted, though `INFO FOR DB` redacts them. It redacts the record-access JWT key. Grant database roles only to trusted operators, and build any export of restricted material in the registry.
- **Restore.** An export that contains a `DEFINE ACCESS` fails to import, because its key is the redaction placeholder. The import is partial. Tables and records arrive, and the access method does not. This corrects the D027 backup result for any database with record access. Export with `--accesses false`, import, and define the access again from its source. A database OWNER can run that import. The new key invalidates earlier tokens, and table permissions still apply after the restore.

These probes cover one record method with a password signin. SIGNUP, refresh tokens, bearer and JWT access, and token expiry are untested. So are lexical search, live queries, events, functions, and computed fields under permissions, and traversals of more than one hop. The [SurrealDB plan](surrealdb-plan.md#10-decisive-acceptance-cases) still asks to pin a tested patch with the documented HNSW permission-filter fix; these probes saw no leak on a 25-row fixture, which does not establish that fix. The start command leaves guest access at the server default, which rejected anonymous queries.

## D039. Register verified packages in SurrealDB and keep their original bytes in an object store

Selected on 22 October 2026 for increment 2 of the [SurrealDB plan](surrealdb-plan.md#9-delivery-increments), the first registry milestone of [Phase 2](master-plan.md#phase-2-deliver-a-durable-research-workspace). [quant_research.registry](../src/quant_research/registry.py) imports a verified portable package, keeps its original bytes in a content-addressed object store, records its contents in SurrealDB under their original identities, and exports the package again from the stored bytes.

The object store is a local directory that stands in for S3. Each file is named by the SHA-256 of its bytes. The store keeps the package's `manifest.json` bytes under the package digest and each artifact's bytes under the artifact digest, so two packages that share an artifact share one file. These bytes remain the hash authority. Export reads the manifest and artifacts from the store, checks each digest, writes `manifest.sha256` from the package digest, and opens the result with `open_package`. A destination that already exists must hold this package. Any other content there, or a destination that cannot be written, raises `RegistryError` and leaves the destination as it was. When two exports race to one new destination, the second finds the first one's directory when it renames its own staged copy, and opens it as an existing destination, so both return the package. Export never encodes a package from decoded records, so its output is byte-identical to the imported package.

SurrealDB holds one SCHEMAFULL table per record kind. Each row is keyed by the identity the package already carries.

| Table | Key | Fields |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `object` | SHA-256 of the bytes | Size of the verified bytes in the object store |
| `specification` | `spec_id` | Specification text, and links to its input, data contract, implementation, and lock objects |
| `trial` | Trial UUID | Lineage UUID, specification, and lineage links in supplied order |
| `attempt` | Attempt UUID | Trial, run UUID, predecessor, start and finish times, outcome, and the objects its result artifacts name |
| `exposure` | Exposure UUID | The event fields, with its material as an object link |
| `package` | Manifest SHA-256 | Manifest object, selected trial if any ([D060](#d060-let-a-package-record-no-selected-trial)), the trials, attempts, and exposures it carries, its named artifacts, coverage, scope, and confirmation |

UUID keys are UUID values, because D027 found that a key of the same UUID text is a different record. Timestamps are SurrealDB datetimes, which hold a package's microsecond UTC times exactly. Packages share trial, attempt, exposure, specification, and object rows, so a retry package that carries its trial's history adds only its new rows. An attempt's `produced` field resolves its result artifact names through the importing package. A later package that maps those names to other bytes therefore conflicts. Each attempt row names its predecessor, so two retries of one failed attempt stay two rows with one predecessor.

Import runs in one client-side transaction. It defines the schema with `IF NOT EXISTS`, reads every row the package needs, and compares each existing row with the row the package implies. A difference is a conflicting immutable identity. Import then cancels the transaction and raises `RegistryError`. The comparison precedes the object writes, so a conflicting package adds no file unless it first lost a commit conflict to the package it conflicts with. Otherwise import writes each object to a temporary file in the store, flushes that file to disk, and renames it over the file named by its digest. After the last object it flushes the store directory, so the new names are on disk too. Only then does it insert the missing rows and commit. A committed row therefore names only objects that are already flushed. A file system error while writing an object, such as a directory at the object's path, raises `RegistryError` and cancels the transaction. A store path that cannot be created as a directory, such as a plain file, raises `RegistryError` before the first transaction. A repeated import finds every row equal and inserts nothing. It still rewrites every object it carries, which restores a missing file and repairs a file whose bytes no longer match its name without changing any row. Export reports such a file as a `RegistryError` that names its digest. Packages that do not carry that object export as before.

D027 measured that snapshot isolation fails the second of two concurrent writes to one record at commit with a retryable `TransactionConflict`. A probe for this decision found the same error when two transactions insert one new record ID, even when the first commits before the second inserts, and when two transactions first define the schema. Import runs the whole transaction at most five times and retries only on that error. A fifth conflict raises `RegistryError`. Two concurrent imports of one package therefore leave one copy. The retry reads the rows the first import wrote and finds them equal.

A package that fails verification raises `PackageError` before the transaction starts, so a tampered package leaves no object, row, or table. Import reads every artifact through `VerifiedPackage.artifacts` and checks the manifest bytes against the opened digest, so a change after opening is also refused, including removal of the package directory. Object files reach the disk before the commit. An interruption between the two leaves unreferenced files and no rows, and a retry completes the import.

Amended on 22 October 2026 to remove unreferenced objects. `remove_unreferenced_objects` removes the object files that no `object` row names, and the `.object-` temporary files that an import killed while writing an object leaves behind. Until an import's rows commit, its flushed objects look unreferenced. Import therefore holds a shared `flock` on the object directory from before its first transaction until it returns or raises. Cleanup takes the same lock exclusively before it reads the `object` rows, so every object it sees belongs to a committed row or to an import that has ended. The kernel releases the lock when a process dies, so a killed import cannot block cleanup. Rows are never deleted, so an object that a row names stays named. Cleanup defines the schema before it reads, because an import interrupted before its first commit leaves objects and no table. It removes only files with the names the registry writes, 64 lowercase hexadecimal digits or a `.object-` prefix. It leaves directories and every other name, but a link with such a name to a file is removed, and its target stays. Pointed at the wrong directory, it still removes files with those names, including another registry's objects.

Amended on 22 October 2026 to store invalidations. `import_invalidation` stores one [D046](#d046-suspend-records-whose-support-an-invalidation-removes) invalidation as a row of the `invalidation` table with its evidence ID, kind, actor, time, and reason. An invalidation carries no ID of its own. The registry keys its row by the SHA-256 of the canonical JSON of its evidence ID, kind, actor, and reason, because suspensions name an invalidation by those fields. Import parses the invalidation before the transaction, so a malformed one raises `InvalidationError` and writes nothing. It then runs the same transaction as package import. It defines the schema, compares the stored row, inserts the row if it is missing, and retries a commit conflict up to five times. Importing the same invalidation again writes nothing. An invalidation with the same evidence ID, kind, actor, and reason but another time conflicts and raises `RegistryError`. Another actor or reason for the same evidence is a second row, so a record's history can name every cause.

Amended on 22 October 2026 to store the review log. `import_ledger` stores each event of a [D019](#d019-admit-lessons-only-through-recorded-review-events) ledger as a row of the `review_event` table, keyed by the event's integer position in the log, counted from 0. The row's `event` field holds the canonical JSON value that `findings.event_value` returns and `findings.parse_event` reads back, the same value that the log's memory heads hash. A loaded ledger therefore has the head of the ledger that was imported. An event carries no identity of its own. A review can repeat an earlier review's fields exactly, and a head depends on the order of the events. The position is the identity, as the file number is in the local logs of [D016](#d016-account-for-resources-in-an-append-only-ledger). The stored order is the log order. Each import must extend the stored log, so the stored rows are always a prefix of a log that `Ledger` accepted, and their times never decrease.

Import runs the same transaction as package import. It compares the stored row at each position with the ledger's event and inserts the positions the store lacks. Importing the same ledger again, or a prefix of the stored log, writes nothing. A ledger whose event differs from the stored event at any position raises `RegistryError` and writes nothing, so two writers cannot fork the log. When two imports race to one new position, the second fails at commit with a `TransactionConflict` and retries. It then finds the first one's row. An equal row writes nothing, and a different row raises.

`load_ledger` reads the stored rows inside one transaction, requires their positions to be exactly 0 to n-1, parses each event, and folds the events into a `Ledger`. A gap or a stray key raises `RegistryError`, because a log missing a declaration or a review still folds into a valid ledger with other restrictions and heads. A row that does not parse raises `ReviewError`. Load then replays every stored invalidation against the folded ledger through the D046 catch-up that `quant_research.invalidation.catch_up` implements. The assessment needs the input digests of each package's trials to find the readers of a vintage. Load reads them in one query from the rows of each registered package whose trials list a stored evidence ID among their inputs. The query follows each package's trial and specification links. Load exports no package and reads no object file. Load stores the suspensions that the catch-up appends at the next positions in the same transaction, dated at the time the caller passes.

The registry stores suspensions because a run context pins a memory head ([D049](#d049-expose-evidence-to-agents-through-typed-tools-bound-to-the-run-context)), and `admissible_lessons` answers from the prefix with that head. A suspension that existed only in a returned ledger would get a new time and a new head at every load, so no pin could name it. A second load appends nothing and writes no row, because each record's history already holds its suspension. Two loads that race with different times both insert the same next position. The second fails at commit, retries, reads the first one's suspensions, appends nothing, and returns the stored log. Load reads the invalidations and selects the reader packages in its transaction's snapshot, so an invalidation or package imported while it runs waits for the next load. When a suspension is due and the caller's time precedes the last stored event, `Ledger` raises `ReviewError` and load writes nothing. The registry has no public catch-up that returns an unstored ledger. A ledger that a caller extends and imports is not caught up until it is loaded again, so a run context takes its memory pin from the ledger that `load_ledger` returns.

Amended on 22 October 2026 to store the worker's run log. `import_run_log` stores each entry of a [D047](#d047-run-generated-signal-code-in-a-locked-down-container) `RunLog` as a row of the `container_run` table, keyed by the entry's file number, counted from 0. The row's `entry` field holds the bytes that `LoggedRun.to_bytes` returns as a SurrealDB bytes value. These are the bytes the run log wrote to the entry's file. Its `run` field holds the entry's run ID as a UUID value. Import first replays every entry through the run log's own checks, so a damaged log raises `WorkerError` and writes nothing. The registry has no encoder of its own. As in the review log, the position is the identity, and each import must extend the stored log.

Import runs the same transaction as package import. Importing the same log again, or a prefix of the stored log, writes nothing, and a log that extends the stored one adds only its new entries. A log whose entry differs from the stored entry at any position raises `RegistryError` and writes nothing. So an entry whose run ID is already stored with other bytes is refused at the stored position. A run ID cannot reach a second position, because a log that repeats a run ID fails the replay before import.

`load_run_log` reads the stored rows, requires their positions to be exactly 0 to n-1, and returns each entry's bytes in log order. A gap or a stray key raises `RegistryError`. Load does not parse the entries. A caller writes entry i as `i.json` in an empty directory and opens it as a `RunLog`, which checks them as it checked the imported log.

No `registry` CLI command exists. The CLI tests run the module with `python -S`, which cannot import the SurrealDB SDK, and a command that connects to a server needs a credential and configuration design that the permission probes have not settled. Callers use the Python functions, as Phase 2's Python interface asks.

These limits are deliberate:

- The cleanup lock is a local file system rule. `flock` is unreliable on network file systems, and an S3 backend needs its own rule for cleanup during an import.
- Import flushes the store directory but not its parents, so a crash just after the first import into a new store can lose the whole directory. A repeated import restores it. Flushing calls `fsync`, which on macOS hands the bytes to the drive without forcing the drive's own cache.
- When the store path did not exist, an import refused for a conflicting identity leaves the directory it created, and any parents it created. Import creates the directory before its first transaction so that it can hold the shared lock across every attempt.
