Creating the directory at the first object write instead would need a lock taken once inside the retry loop and held after it. Removing the directory after a refusal would race a concurrent import that has already opened it. The refused import adds no object to it unless it first lost a commit conflict, and the next import uses it.
- Import defines the schema on first use. A changed field needs a migration. [D060](#d060-let-a-package-record-no-selected-trial) added the first, which the import transaction runs.
- Lineage links are fields, not graph edges. Increment 4 adds relations and traversal.
- The registry connects as root through the local server configuration in `store.py`. Non-root users, record permissions, and an S3 backend are not implemented, and [D038](#d038-restrict-reads-through-record-access-and-make-denied-writes-throw) records the permission behavior they must follow.
- The registry holds invalidations, review events, and run log entries, but not acceptance or steering events. No event lifts a stored invalidation.
- Review events keep only their caller-declared times. The registry adds none of the recorded times that [D019](#d019-admit-lessons-only-through-recorded-review-events) plans for the store.
- Each ledger or run log import and each load reads the whole stored log.
- The registry holds one run log per database. The campaign runner and the `run_signal` tool must append to one run log directory for the registry to hold the runs of both. `RunLog` allows concurrent writers. Another log differs from the stored entry at position 0, so its import raises `RegistryError`.
- `load_run_log` returns the stored bytes without parsing them and does not check a row's `run` field against its entry. Import writes both from one checked entry, and nothing updates a row.
- `Ledger` does not check the types of declared labels and providers. A declaration whose label is not text imports, and then every load raises `ReviewError`.
- `load_ledger` trusts the package, trial, and specification rows for each package's inputs and does not check them against the manifest object. Import builds every row from the verified package and refuses a stored row that differs before it commits, and nothing updates or deletes a row. A direct database edit or database corruption could still withdraw a package that never read the evidence, or miss one that did. A corrupt manifest or artifact does not stop `load_ledger`, and an export of that package still raises `RegistryError` naming the object. A check of the rows against the manifest object would let one corrupt manifest block every load, and no import can repair a manifest when the registry holds the only copy of the package.
- `load_ledger` sees only registered packages. A record that cites an unregistered package that read a withdrawn vintage keeps that support.

## D040. Retrieve exactly over a pinned eligible corpus

Selected on 22 October 2026 for Phase 3 retrieval. [quant_research.retrieval](../src/quant_research/retrieval.py) computes the exact eligible corpus for a run and answers a query over all of it. Phase 3 measures later SurrealDB lexical, vector, and graph retrieval against this baseline.

A corpus pins the published source generations that runs may search. It maps each admitted generation ID to its manifest digest and admits at most one generation per source occurrence. Its ID is the SHA-256 of its canonical JSON. `publish_corpus` opens and verifies each generation, then stores the corpus once under `corpora/` in the source store. A run context's `generation` names a corpus ID, not a source generation. A new extractor or chunker version, or a new source, therefore enters only a new corpus, and runs pinned to an older corpus keep their results.

`eligible_units` returns the exact eligible corpus. It reads through `published_generations` ([D036](#d036-filter-source-reads-through-the-trusted-run-context)), so each unit's labels are within the run's clearances and its `source_available_at` is at or before the cutoff. It keeps the chunks of the generations in the pinned corpus, ordered by source availability, generation ID, and offset. A corpus member that is missing from the store raises, so a damaged store never shrinks the result. A readable member whose digest differs from the pin also raises. A publication that stops before its rename leaves only a staging directory, which `publish_corpus` cannot admit, so partly processed output is never searchable. Restrictions come only from the source declaration, so a unit's text cannot grant access. `corpus_members` returns the pinned generations themselves under the same filter and checks, including a generation whose chunker produced no chunks, and `eligible_units` takes their chunks.

`retrieve` answers a query over every eligible unit, with no result limit. Query text has no NUL and encodes as UTF-8, so every query that runs can be recorded. A query names its mode and has nonblank text, because a blank identifier would match at every word boundary:

- An identifier query returns each unit that contains the identifier exactly, with no case folding or normalization and no word character directly before or after it. Units keep corpus order.
- A lexical query scores units with BM25, where k1 is 1.2, b is 0.75, and idf is ln(1 + (N − n + 0.5) / (n + 0.5)). Tokens are the runs of word characters in the casefolded text, and each distinct query term counts once. N, n, and the average unit length come from the eligible units only, so restricted or later material cannot change an uncleared run's ranking. Each unit with a positive score is returned, highest score first, with ties in corpus order.

Each result records what was supplied: the query, the context's identity, the corpus ID, the cutoff, and the returned units in order. Each unit names its occurrence and generation and keeps its code-point span and text. The context's identity is `RunContext.identity` ([D045](#d045-read-approved-datasets-through-a-timing-and-access-adapter)), a digest of its authority, so the record names no label. The record encodes to canonical JSON, and decoding and encoding it again gives identical bytes. `replay` takes a trusted context with the recorded identity, runs the query again, and raises if any recorded field or unit differs. A record cannot widen access, because replay refuses any other context and a changed record fails the comparison.

These limits are deliberate:

- Scores are floating-point numbers and are not recorded. Order near an exact tie can differ on a platform whose logarithm rounds differently. Replay is checked on one platform.
- The tokenizer, the identifier boundary, and the BM25 parameters define the baseline. Word characters are Python's `\w`, which excludes combining marks, so an identifier matches before a combining accent. A SurrealDB analyzer must reproduce them, or the comparison must report the difference.
- Eligibility uses `source_available_at`, not a generation's `recorded_at`. Output produced today from historical material is eligible as a replay transformation, as the [SurrealDB plan](surrealdb-plan.md#4-time-and-causal-eligibility) allows.
- Each call opens and verifies every published generation. The baseline is exact, not indexed.
- The record holds the units retrieval returned, not a model submission. Context manifests and disclosure events wait for the agent tool contract.
- The module imports the private generation reader of [sources.py](../src/quant_research/sources.py) and the exclusive file writer of [records.py](../src/quant_research/records.py) that sources also uses, so the corpus record and the generations share one store format.

## D041. Check outbound provider content against the run and a versioned provider policy

Selected on 22 October 2026 for Phases 0 and 3 of the [master plan](master-plan.md). [quant_research.providers](../src/quant_research/providers.py) is the one path from platform content to a model adapter. A `Gateway` holds one route per provider, which pairs the provider's data policy with its adapter. The gateway is the only writer of the call log, which a `CallLog` keeps in a local directory. It charges each call it sends to the [resource ledger](#d016-account-for-resources-in-an-append-only-ledger) through a `LedgerStore`. A call that a policy refuses sends nothing and is not charged.

A `ProviderPolicy` states which restriction labels one provider may receive and whether the provider retains content. Its version is the SHA-256 digest of its canonical JSON, so a change to either term gives a new version.

Before each dispatch, the gateway derives the restrictions of the request's materials with the [D025](#d025-carry-restrictions-with-material-and-authority-with-the-run) rule and applies the run context's send check, then the policy. Content goes to a provider only when every one of these holds:

- The run clears every label, and the content was available at the run's cutoff.
- The run permits the provider, and the material's own approval names it.
- The provider's policy lists every label.

The policy is a ceiling on labels. D025 rejected a provider-to-label matrix on the context, because material that ingestion failed to label would reach every permitted provider. The ceiling does not bring that back, because the material's positive approval still applies.

A refused call is recorded with its reason codes and zero usage. Its adapter is never invoked, and the ledger is not charged. The gateway then tries the next fallback in the role assignment, and the fallback passes the same check. A provider without a route raises `ProviderError` before any dispatch, because a missing policy is operator misconfiguration, not a content refusal. Before each model in the chain, the gateway also raises `ProviderError` when the ledger has not admitted the attempt or has already released it. A fallback therefore never runs after its attempt closes, and all spend passes an admission decision, as D016 requires. An attempt can still close while an adapter runs, and the ledger accepts that call's charge as late billing. The gateway replays the store and asks `Ledger.is_open`, so the ledger's own fold answers that question.

An adapter receives a `Cleared` value with the model record, the texts, and the requested output schema. Only the gateway creates one, as only `issue_context` creates a run context. An adapter returns a reply, a failure, or a cancellation, each with the usage it observed, and it does not raise for provider-side trouble.

A request may declare a flat schema of string, integer, float, and boolean fields. The gateway parses the reply exactly and does not coerce it. Each of these makes the call `malformed_output`:

- The reply is not one JSON object, or it repeats a key.
- A key is missing or extra.
- A number is not finite.
- A value's JSON type differs from the declared type. An integer does not fill a float field, and a boolean does not fill an integer field.

A malformed reply and a provider failure are still charged, and the next fallback runs. A completed reply returns as material with the request's derived restrictions. Text inside the reply cannot change them. A reply that claims a clearance stays restricted like its inputs, and a narrower provider refuses it.

Cancellation is a `threading.Event` that the adapter checks. A cancelled call is recorded and charged with the usage the adapter reported, and it ends the fallback chain. The gateway never releases the attempt, because the attempt's owner decides its outcome.

Each call record names the call, the attempt, the role, the model record, the policy version, the outcome, any refusal reasons, and the usage. The call ID is also the ledger charge ID. Before an adapter runs, the gateway stores a dispatch entry that names the call, the attempt, the role, the model record, and the policy version. A refused call has no dispatch. When a call has a dispatch, its record must come after it and repeat its fields with a sent outcome, so replay refuses a record that changes the model or claims that a dispatched call was refused. A dispatch that the log cannot store, for example for a role with a lone surrogate, which `parse_text` accepts and canonical JSON cannot encode, or on a full disk, raises before any content leaves. A `CallLog` keeps the records in an existing directory, as `LedgerStore` keeps its events. Entry *i* is the canonical JSON file `i.json` with schema version 1, created once. Every operation replays the stored entries first and checks every field again. An append also reads its new entry back as replay reads it before writing anything, so an entry that would fail the next open raises `ProviderError` and stores nothing. A gap, a stray file, noncanonical bytes, a repeated entry, or a conflicting entry fails the open.

The gateway stores a sent call's record after its dispatch and before it charges the ledger. A record holds every field of its charge, while a charge lacks the model record, the policy version, and the outcome. Only this order therefore lets a later open rebuild the missing half. Before it charges anything, opening a `Gateway` raises `ProviderError` and writes nothing when a dispatch or call record names an attempt that its ledger never reserved. The gateway checks that an attempt is open before every model, and a release keeps the reservation, so its own log names only attempts that its ledger reserved. Such a log was written against another ledger, or its ledger lost events. The open reads the log before the ledger, so a call that another gateway logs meanwhile still finds its reservation. The two stores cannot be written in one step, so a crash between the writes leaves a record without its charge. Opening a `Gateway` charges every sent record that has neither a ledger charge nor a stored refusal, and charging an identical record again appends nothing. The gateway's own writes never leave a charge without its record. The ledger cannot tell an orphaned call charge from any other charge, so no rule looks for one.

When the ledger refuses a charge, for example because the campaign's usage would no longer sum exactly, the adapter has already run. The gateway stores a `ChargeRefused` entry with the call ID and the ledger's reason, then raises `LedgerError`. The gateway treats a stored refusal as final, and a later open skips that call. A release that lowers the campaign's exposure can make the same charge fit, but the gateway does not try it again. A crash before the refusal is stored leaves a record with neither, so the next open tries the charge again, stores the refusal, and raises. An open raises at the first such refusal, so each pending refusal takes one more open. Only the ledger's fold decides a refusal. The gateway checks each charge against a replayed ledger before the store writes it, because the store also raises `LedgerError` when it cannot write. A failed ledger write leaves the record for the next open to charge. So does a refusal that another writer causes between the check and the write, and the next open stores that refusal.

These limits are deliberate:

- No rule reads the retention flag. Phase 0 decides which labels need a provider that does not retain content.
- The policies, labels, and approvals in the tests are test policies.
- `ScriptedProvider` is the only adapter. It is a deterministic local fake with no network, and the tests run two instances under different policies. Real adapters, PydanticAI, and the internal model SDK wait for Phase 0.
- A crash while an adapter runs, or a record write that fails after the adapter returns, leaves the call's dispatch without a record. The dispatch shows which model received the content under which policy, but the outcome and usage are unknown, so nothing charges the call. Opening a gateway leaves such a dispatch alone, because it cannot tell it from a call still running in another gateway on the same log.
- The check compares attempts, not ledger identities, and it runs only at open. A ledger that reserved every attempt the log names, such as a copy of the right directory, passes. An empty log opens with any ledger, so two gateways that open it with different ledgers can both write to it, and then neither ledger can reopen it. Keeping each log with its own ledger directory is still the operator's job.
- Before this check, a gateway opened with another ledger stored each sent call's charge as refused. The gateway treats that refusal as final under the right ledger too, so the right ledger never receives the charge.
- The call log shares the ledger store's limits. Writes are not synced to disk, and an edit that still passes replay, such as deleting trailing entries, goes undetected.
- `Cleared` stops accidental bypass, not deliberate forgery. Integration PRs must audit any direct adapter call, as D025 requires for `issue_context`.

## D042. Run each campaign as one Temporal workflow that owns its logs

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the durable execution in [Phase 2 of the master plan](master-plan.md#phase-2-deliver-a-durable-research-workspace). [quant_research.execution](../src/quant_research/execution.py) replaces the [D026](#d026-qualify-temporal-for-durable-campaign-execution) probe with the first campaign runner. It takes a revision from [campaign specifications](#d013-version-campaign-specifications-as-content-addressed-revisions) and runs the revision's declared jobs as one Temporal workflow.

The data shape comes first. A `Plan` holds the revision's canonical file text and its jobs. A `Job` is one logical execution, which is one trial with one run in the [acceptance log](#d030-accept-results-through-explicit-events). It names a body that the worker declares, an argument, and a declared cost in compute seconds. Attempt n runs as its own activity with the ID `<job>/<n>`, and server retries stay off, as D026 found necessary. The workflow returns a `Summary` of its status and the compute seconds charged, and a query returns the same summary while the run is open. `record` reads a closed run's `Record` in parts, per [D073](#d073-bound-the-campaign-runners-per-event-cost-and-payloads). The record names the workflow ID, the run ID, the status, and the compute seconds charged. It has one link per attempt, which names the activity ID, the acceptance attempt UUID, the revision the attempt ran under, and how Temporal ended the activity. It also carries the acceptance and steering logs as their canonical text.

The workflow is the only writer of three logs. It keeps the [resource ledger](#d016-account-for-resources-in-an-append-only-ledger), the acceptance log, and the [steering log](#d031-record-campaign-steering-in-an-append-only-log) in memory, and Temporal rebuilds them from history after a worker restart. Workflow code takes identities from `workflow.uuid4()` and times from `workflow.now()`, so a replay rebuilds the same logs. This meets the single-writer limit of D016 and D030. A revision enters the workflow and each attempt as its canonical file text, and the workflow parses each attempt's report with the package parsers. Temporal's JSON converter cannot decode the PEP 695 aliases in the campaign and package types, so typed arguments would fail.

The workflow ID is the campaign ID. A repeated start of a running campaign returns its run, and a start after the run closes is refused, through the D026 policies. A campaign therefore has one run, and a methodology change during it arrives as a steer. Temporal refuses the second start only while the namespace retains the closed run, so the research record must keep campaign IDs single-use. `start` parses the revision as the workflow will. It refuses job and body names outside `[A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,100}`, because the names enter activity IDs, ledger roles, and acceptance reasons. It also refuses duplicate job names, more jobs than the revision's `max_trials`, costs that are not nonnegative integers, and job arguments that are not valid UTF-8. `start` stores a digest of the plan in the run's memo, and a repeated start with a different revision or different jobs is refused instead of joining the run.

Before it schedules an attempt, the runner reserves the job's declared cost in the ledger and charges it. The budget's compute-second limit is the revision's `max_compute_seconds`. Its failures-per-run limit is `max_attempts`, which D013 left undefined and this runner reads as attempts per logical execution. A refusal of compute seconds ends the campaign as `budget_exhausted`, and the refused attempt is never scheduled. A refusal of failure slots alone means that the job used its attempts. That job stays unaccepted, and the campaign moves to the next job. Failed, interrupted, and cancelled attempts keep their charge, because the ledger releases them as failed.

An attempt's report carries the body's outcome and the time the attempt finished. When a body raises, the worker reports a failed outcome with the exception's `repr`. The runner records each reported outcome as a completion. Temporal cannot refuse a report, so the runner records a completed report that it cannot decode or that the acceptance log refuses, from a body or an external reporter, as a failed outcome. A failed report whose error details cannot be decoded fails only its attempt, per [D073](#d073-bound-the-campaign-runners-per-event-cost-and-payloads). That outcome names the refusal and is dated when the workflow read the report, and a malformed completion cannot fail every workflow task. It accepts the first attempt of a job whose outcome is not failed, with the actor `runner` and a reason. A failed outcome leads to a retry. A Temporal failure, heartbeat timeout, or cancellation records no completion, so that attempt stays visible and unaccepted, as D030 requires for an interrupted execution. The next attempt names the previous one as its predecessor. Temporal refuses a late report for a closed attempt and a second report for the same attempt with `NOT_FOUND`, so neither reaches the acceptance log.

Researchers steer the run through two Temporal updates. `control` records a pause, resume, cancel, or annotation. `steer` records a methodology steer to a child revision. Each validator applies the event to the steering log and discards the result, so the log's own rules refuse a transition, the caller receives the error, and nothing enters history. A pause lets the running attempt finish and schedules nothing until a resume. A cancel stops the running attempt with `WAIT_CANCELLATION_COMPLETED`, and the workflow completes with the status `cancelled`. An external attempt sends no heartbeat that could deliver the cancel, so the runner cancels it with `TRY_CANCEL` and does not wait. The attempt ends cancelled at once, and Temporal refuses its later report. A steer must name a child of a revision that the run holds. Later attempts run under the child, and earlier attempts keep the revision they ran under. A steer whose child changes `limits` is refused, because the ledger fixes the budget when the run starts.

Two alternatives were rejected. The probe carried steering in signals. A signal cannot refuse a command, so an invalid one would either fail the workflow task or disappear. Logs written to files by activities were also rejected. They add a second writer and a crash window between an activity's write and the workflow's decision, while history already persists the workflow's state. For the same reason the runner keeps an in-memory `Ledger` that history rebuilds and does not write the D016 `LedgerStore`, whose file I/O workflow code cannot perform.

[The runner tests](../tests/test_execution.py) use the D026 dev-server fixture. They cover a killed worker, a repeated start, duplicate and late reports, a failed report with undecodable details, a record over the payload limit, pause and steer, cancel, budget exhaustion, a job that uses all its attempts, and generated code through the [D047](#d047-run-generated-signal-code-in-a-locked-down-container) worker. D026 did not probe updates, so these tests are their first check on the pinned server.

These limits are deliberate:

- Costs are declared, not measured. No job reaches a cluster scheduler, and cancellation stops only a local activity. Both wait for the compute path that [Q005](open-questions.md#q005-which-infrastructure-and-provider-assumptions-are-correct) leaves open.
- The logs live only in Temporal history and the run's record. Nothing persists them in SurrealDB or imports them into a registry.
- A synchronous body such as replay or generated code runs in a worker thread, which a cancel cannot stop. The runner records the cancel, and the thread finishes on its own. A generated candidate's container stops at its own wall-clock limit.
- A replay package mints its own trial, run, and attempt IDs. Only the accepted outcome's detail names the package, and the package does not name the runner's IDs.
- An external job has no heartbeat timeout, so a lost reporter is found only by the 10-minute start-to-close timeout.
- [D073](#d073-bound-the-campaign-runners-per-event-cost-and-payloads) records the remaining limits on replay time, history size, and report size.
- A cancel through Temporal's own workflow cancellation bypasses the steering log and ends the run without a record. Researchers cancel through `control`.

## D043. Locate paper elements in published extractions

Selected on 22 October 2026 for Phase 3 paper ingestion. [quant_research.locators](../src/quant_research/locators.py) finds equations, tables, figures, and claim sentences in the text of a published extraction. It publishes them as a locator generation beside the chunk generations of [D018](#d018-publish-complete-source-generations-from-a-file-store).

A locator generation names an extraction and a versioned locator step, as a chunk generation names an extraction and a chunker. Its ID derives from the extraction ID and the locator's name and version, so a new locator version is a new generation. The manifest records the extraction's text digest and code-point length and lists each location with its kind, start, end, and text. Locations are sorted by start, end, and kind, and they may nest, so inline math inside a claim sentence is two locations. `LocatorGeneration.ref` names one location as `<generation ID>:<kind>:<start>-<end>`. The generation ID already carries the extraction and the locator version. `LocatorGeneration.lookup` returns the location a ref names from an index built once per generation object, and `ref` checks a location through the same index, so neither scans the locations.

Publication follows D018. `publish_locations` requires a published extraction, whose binding a chunk generation writes, and raises `SourceError` without one. It reruns the declared extractor on the stored blob and compares the text's digest and length with the binding. Different text under the same extractor label raises `IdentityConflict`. The module stages the complete manifest privately and renames it into `locations/`. A retry returns the existing generation with its first `recorded_at`. Different locations under the same locator version raise `IdentityConflict`, and a failed run publishes nothing. `published_locations` verifies every locator generation and returns those the run context may read under [D036](#d036-filter-source-reads-through-the-trusted-run-context). A reader checks each span against the recorded length without the extractor, and each location's text must fill its span. Both modules call the same private publication and read helpers in `sources.py`.

Version 1 of the `markdown-elements` locator reads the output of the UTF-8 text and markdown pipeline. It skips fenced code and inline code. An equation is `$$...$$`, `\[...\]`, a LaTeX `equation`, `align`, `gather`, or `multline` environment, `\(...\)`, or `$...$` under pandoc's rule, so `$5 and $10` is not math. Math does not cross a blank line. A table is a markdown pipe table with a delimiter row. A figure is an inline markdown image, or a paragraph that starts with `Figure N` or `Fig. N` and a period or colon. The locator reads images before inline math, so an image inside `$...$` is a figure, and inline math inside alt text is not an equation. Alt text and titles give no claim cues. A claim is any other prose sentence that contains a cue from one fixed list: a percentage, percent or basis points after a number, "t-stat", "p-value", "significant", "outperform", or a finding phrase such as "we find" or "results show". A period after a listed abbreviation such as "e.g." or "Fig." does not end a sentence. Any change to these rules or lists needs a new locator version.

Version 2 keeps version 1's rules and lists and reads six more forms. A lone CR ends a line, as LF and CRLF do. Indented code is skipped: a run of lines indented by four columns after a blank line or the document start, unless the nearest preceding unindented line starts a list item, because pandoc indents a list item's later paragraphs by four spaces. A closed HTML comment is skipped anywhere outside code, across blank lines included. A figure may also be a full, collapsed, or shortcut reference-style image whose label has a one-line link reference definition. Labels match case-insensitively after whitespace collapses, and definitions are skipped. A blank label defines nothing, and a `[^label]:` line is a footnote, not a definition. A table may sit inside a blockquote when every line carries the quote marker. Its span starts after the header line's marker, and its other lines keep theirs. A setext heading, a paragraph after a blank line or the document start that an `=` or `-` underline follows, is skipped like an ATX heading unless one of its lines starts with a list or quote marker. `MARKDOWN_LOCATOR` stays version 1, so existing generations and the specifications that pin them keep their bytes. `MARKDOWN_LOCATOR_V2` publishes its own generations under new IDs.

These limits are deliberate:

- As with chunks, checksums detect ordinary damage. A reader cannot detect a span and its checksum rewritten together, because the store keeps no extracted text.
- Claim detection matches cues and does not classify meaning. It misses a claim without a cue and marks a descriptive sentence that has one. A figure caption is never a claim.
- There is no PDF parsing. A figure is found only as an inline markdown image or a caption.
- Version 1 reads common markdown forms only. It misses reference-style images and tables inside a blockquote, reads setext headings, HTML comments, and indented code as prose, and treats only LF and CRLF as line endings.
- Version 2 still misreads less common forms. Indented code inside a list item or a blockquote, or directly after a heading, reads as prose. A list item's lazy continuation line ends the list for that rule, as does a continuation paragraph indented by fewer than four columns, so a later four-column paragraph of that item reads as code. A setext heading inside a blockquote or a list item reads as prose. HTML other than comments reads as prose with its tags, an unclosed comment reads as prose, and a code span opened inside a comment wins over the comment. A link reference definition that continues on the next line is not read, a definition line inside a paragraph still counts, and a backslash-escaped image is still a figure. Unicode line separators other than LF and CR split paragraphs, as in version 1, but no line rule ends a line at them.
- A [reproduction specification](#d020-fix-the-reproduction-comparison-before-results) checks its equation, table, figure, and claim refs against a locator generation only when it pins one. Chunk generations have no ref form, so its passage refs stay opaque.

## D044. Shard the full suite and keep the smoke suite small

Chosen on 22 October 2026, when five to ten sessions verified at once on one machine and the full suite had become the main wait. The repository-gate tests took most of a full run. Much of their time went to fixture commits and pushes that ran the commit and push hooks without testing them, so those fixtures now skip the hooks.

`make verify` collects the snapshot's tests once to count them, then starts four pytest processes. Each shard collects the whole suite and applies pytest's usual `-k` and `-m` selection. The [shard plugin](../tools/shards.py) then keeps every fourth remaining test by position, and a shard that selects a different number of tests fails the suite. Shards select by position because some parametrize IDs include values generated at import, so the same test has a different node ID in each process. Each shard starts through the same isolated bootstrap as a single run. It loads pytest from the prepared environment before adding the captured root and source paths, and it runs with `-B -I -S`. Every suite runs with its own `--basetemp` in a private temporary directory, and each shard gets a separate one, so shards and concurrent sessions never share pytest's per-user temporary root. Shard output prints after every shard finishes, and the suite fails when collection or any shard fails. Smoke and coverage still run in one process.

pytest-xdist was not adopted. It stops on this suite with "Different tests were collected" for the same parametrize IDs. Its workers also start without the isolated bootstrap, so a captured `pytest.py` at the snapshot root runs in each worker, and restoring the bootstrap order would mean patching a private xdist path list. The new dependency would also change `uv.lock` and make every open PR prepare a new environment.

The smoke suite keeps every module's smoke tests and three gate checks. They show that hooks ignore the working runner and Makefile, that raw export keeps Git's bytes, and that Make metadata never becomes shell code. The other gate tests run only in the full suite, so `make verify` catches a runner change that breaks them, and the commit hook does not.

These costs are accepted:

- Shards are balanced by collection position, not by duration.
- A nested full suite inside a gate test pays for one extra collection.
- Each shard starts its own Temporal and SurrealDB servers and builds its own gate fixture template.
- Shards assume every process collects tests in the same order. The count check catches a different number of tests, not a different order, so a parametrize over an unordered collection would let one test run twice and another not at all.
- A test that leaks state into a later test fails only when both land in the same shard.

## D045. Read approved datasets through a timing-and-access adapter

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the [Phase 3](master-plan.md#phase-3-ground-research-in-papers-code-and-data) requirement that agent use of a catalogued dataset goes through an adapter that enforces timing and access rules. [quant_research.datasets](../src/quant_research/datasets.py) is the adapter that a [D024](#d024-catalog-datasets-as-a-log-of-evidenced-transitions) approval names, as `quant_research.datasets/v1`. It reads a local fixture format, because no real dataset is approved yet.

A fixture is one canonical JSON file per dataset, named by the dataset ID. Each row version has a key, a revision number, a `published_at` time, an `arrived_at` time, and a JSON object of values. These fields carry the three vintage aspects whose evidence approval requires: publication, revision, and arrival. A version becomes available at the later of its publication and its arrival. The SHA-256 of the file is the dataset's vintage. The parser accepts exactly these fields, so a fixture cannot carry restriction labels. A read checks every row before it filters, so a malformed row raises even when the cutoff would exclude it.

`read` takes a catalog, a store directory, a dataset ID, and a trusted run context. It returns the supplied rows with a record, or a refusal. It refuses before it opens the fixture when the ID is not a logical name or not in the catalog, when the dataset's latest event is not an approval, when the approval names another adapter, or when the run is not cleared for every access label in the documentation in force. A refusal for a documented, validated, or revoked dataset names that status and the missing agent-use approval. An uncleared refusal names no label, as [D025](#d025-carry-restrictions-with-material-and-authority-with-the-run) requires. An argument that is not a `RunContext` raises, as in [D036](#d036-filter-source-reads-through-the-trusted-run-context).

Approval is the current state of the catalog passed to the read. The read compares no catalog time with the run's cutoff, because the cutoff is a simulated information horizon and catalog events record governance in wall-clock time. A run with a 2015 cutoff can therefore read a dataset approved in October 2026. A revocation takes effect for every read that is passed a catalog recording it. The catalog does not schedule transitions, so an event dated in the future is in force once it is recorded.

The documented access labels become the dataset's restriction labels, which resolves D024's plain labels for adapter reads. Each row version gets `Restrictions` with those labels and its availability, and the run's read check decides it. For each key, the read supplies the highest revision that the check allows, and it omits a key with no allowed revision without a reason. A later revision therefore never reaches a run whose cutoff precedes it. Supplied rows are ordered by key.

Each supplied read returns a `ReadRecord` of the dataset, its vintage, the catalog digest, the cutoff, the run context identity, and the supplied key and revision pairs in order. `RunContext.identity` is the SHA-256 of the context's canonical clearances, cutoff, generation, and providers, and of its memory pin when it has one ([D025](#d025-carry-restrictions-with-material-and-authority-with-the-run)), so equal authority has one identity. The record round-trips through canonical JSON bytes. `ReadRecord.replay` takes fixture bytes, checks their SHA-256 against the vintage, and returns the recorded rows in recorded order. It needs no catalog or context, because its caller already holds the bytes.

These limits are deliberate:

- The store holds one file per dataset. Replay needs the bytes of the recorded vintage, so it raises after the file changes until a registry keeps earlier vintages. [D052](#d052-ingest-the-pilots-public-data-into-a-vintage-addressed-store) replaces this store with one that keeps every vintage.
- Row values are opaque. The read does not check them against the documented fields.
- Labels apply to a whole dataset. Row-level labels and provider sends are not implemented.
- The catalog digest is evidence. Replay does not reopen the catalog.
- Test fixtures and approvals are synthetic. Real datasets wait for the Phase 0 inventory.

## D046. Suspend records whose support an invalidation removes

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the Phase 5 correction increment. [quant_research.invalidation](../src/quant_research/invalidation.py) takes one invalidation of external evidence and reports every claim, finding, and lesson whose supporting links reach it. An invalidation names a SHA-256 evidence ID exactly as records cite it, a kind, an actor, a time, and a reason. The kinds are a corrected or revoked dataset vintage, an invalid experiment package, and a retracted source. Records cite a package by its manifest digest and a source by the ID they already use. A vintage is a digest that package specifications list among their input digests. A vintage invalidation also withdraws every package that has a trial declaring that digest, because a package's selected trial was chosen against all of its trials. The module matches these IDs literally and reads no catalog, source store, or package. The caller passes each package digest with the input digests that its trials declare. The catalog's vintage evidence digests identify timing evidence, not dataset bytes, so packages cannot be joined to them.

A record is affected when one of its supporting links cites withdrawn evidence or an affected record. It keeps support when another supporting link remains, meaning evidence outside the withdrawn set or a standing record that this invalidation leaves supported. The caller also passes every other invalidation in force, in any order, and the assessment replays them against the same ledger first. It orders them by time, then by evidence ID, kind, actor, and reason, so the result does not depend on the order the caller gives. Their withdrawn evidence no longer counts as remaining support, so successive invalidations add up. A record that one of them leaves without support counts as lost until its history holds that invalidation's suspension. From then on the ledger's status decides, so a later review restores the record. A record that cited two withdrawn items loses support once both are gone, even though it kept support after the first. Contradicting links neither carry nor remove support. The report lists every affected record with its kind, a finding's own outcome, the links that carry the invalidation, and whether support remains. It encodes to canonical JSON and parses back to the same bytes.

[The review lifecycle](../src/quant_research/findings.py) gains a suspend action and a suspended status. Applying a report appends a suspension to each affected record that has lost support and is not retracted or superseded. The suspension's reason names the invalidation's kind, evidence ID, and reason. Records and earlier events do not change. A record already suspended by another invalidation receives the new suspension too, so its history names every cause. Applying the same report twice appends nothing. A suspension is dated at the invalidation's time unless the caller gives a later one. To catch up a ledger that has moved on, the caller reassesses each invalidation in force in that same order against the current ledger, passing the ones before it, and applies each report dated now. `catch_up` does this for a caller-declared time and repeats the pass until a pass appends nothing. A later invalidation's suspension can remove the last support that an earlier invalidation left, so a later pass adds the earlier cause to the record's history. Re-review returns a suspended record to reviewed, and a lesson then needs a new admission. Admission refuses a lesson when every supporting link cites a suspended record, so re-review starts from the evidence and works up to the lesson.

Suspension narrows the whole-set rule of [D019](#d019-admit-lessons-only-through-recorded-review-events). A suspended supporting record removes no support from a record that cites it while another supporting link of that record still holds. A lesson with a second, independent finding therefore stays eligible, and the report lists it as affected so a reviewer can reconsider it. Every other record reached through supporting links must still stand, including the records beneath a suspended record, so a suspension never hides a retraction or supersession. A record that is not suspended loses support when every supporting link cites a suspended record, and so do its citers. Once that record is suspended too, as `suspend` does for each record left without support, a citer with other support stands again. The ledger treats external evidence as standing, as D019 does. Only an assessment knows which evidence an invalidation withdrew, so a suspension made by hand cannot account for it. A record admitted on withdrawn evidence after its invalidation therefore stays eligible until the next assessment that replays that invalidation. Retraction and supersession keep the whole-set rule, because they record a reviewer's judgment that the cited record is wrong or replaced. A suspension records only that the evidence beneath a record changed.

The report keeps an invalid implementation apart from a valid negative result. A finding that rejects an idea keeps that outcome in its record. When its package is invalidated, the report lists the finding with that outcome beside the invalid-package kind, and the finding is suspended. A rejection whose package still stands is not listed, and its lessons stay eligible. Recording a new finding with the `invalid_implementation` outcome and superseding the original still follows D019.

The module trusts each invalidation's actor, time, and reason. It does not inspect evidence or decide whether a correction changed a result. It does not follow lineage links between packages or expand a retracted source to its generations.

Amended on 22 October 2026 to persist invalidations in the [registry](#d039-register-verified-packages-in-surrealdb-and-keep-their-original-bytes-in-an-object-store). An invalidation's stored identity is its evidence ID, kind, actor, and reason, the fields that name its suspensions. Two invalidations that differ only in time are one invalidation, and the registry refuses the second. The registry's `load_ledger` replays every stored invalidation against the stored review log and stores the suspensions it appends, so a later load appends nothing.

Stored invalidations are permanent. No event lifts one, so an invalidation recorded in error keeps suspending each new record that cites its evidence, and reviewers restore those records one by one through re-review. This is accepted while no real invalidation has proved wrong. If one does, a later change may add an append-only lift event that names the invalidation by its stored identity, with its own actor, time, and reason. `load_ledger` would then replay only the invalidations that no lift names. Suspensions made before the lift would stay in each record's history, and re-review would restore those records as it restores any suspended record. Restoring records automatically is rejected, because a status change would then have no recorded review, which [D019](#d019-admit-lessons-only-through-recorded-review-events) requires. Deleting the invalidation's row is rejected, because registry rows are never deleted.

## D047. Run generated signal code in a locked-down container

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the generated-Python extension of [Phase 4](master-plan.md#phase-4-complete-a-bounded-autonomous-campaign), whose workers receive limited inputs and no evaluator or control-plane credentials. [quant_research.worker](../src/quant_research/worker.py) runs candidate source in a Docker container and returns only output that passes host checks. Process limits on macOS could not isolate credentials. There, `setrlimit` rejects memory caps, and a child process can read its parent's environment. A container gives the candidate its own filesystem, network namespace, and environment.

`run` takes candidate source, a catalog, a store, a dataset ID, a run context, and limits. It reads the slice through the [D045](#d045-read-approved-datasets-through-a-timing-and-access-adapter) adapter first, so a refused read starts no container and returns the adapter's reason. It writes the source and the supplied rows to a host temporary directory, which the container sees read-only at `/job`. The slice file is canonical JSON with each supplied row's key, revision, publication and arrival times, and values.

The host creates each container with these settings:

- `--network none`, so DNS, TCP, and UDP fail.
- A read-only root filesystem, a size-limited tmpfs at `/tmp` for scratch, and a tmpfs at `/output` for the one output file. `--ipc none` removes `/dev/shm`, and a read-only tmpfs covers `/dev/mqueue`, because both are otherwise writable.
- User 65534, `--cap-drop ALL`, `no-new-privileges`, and Docker's default seccomp profile.
- `--pids-limit`, `--memory` with an equal `--memory-swap`, and `--cpus`.
- No host environment. The image entrypoint starts the candidate under `env -i` with only `PATH`.
- `--pull never`, the image ID in place of the tag, no log driver, and a label that names the run's job directory. Tests and people find a run's containers by this label, even when runs share a Docker daemon or run the same source. The worker never looks containers up by label.

The entrypoint runs `python -I /job/candidate.py` and discards its standard output. When the candidate exits 0, the entrypoint writes a tar of `/output` to standard output. The host reads that stream against a wall-clock deadline and a byte bound. After a timeout or an oversized stream, it removes the container at once. Otherwise it reads the exit code and the out-of-memory flag from `docker inspect`. The host names the container before it runs `docker create`, and it force-removes the container by that name and deletes the temporary directory, also after a refusal or a host exception. The daemon finishes a create it has received even after its CLI dies, and a `docker rm` that dies before it reaches the daemon removes nothing. So the host waits for every docker command it has started, also after a host exception, and raises the exception once the command exits. Python runs signal handlers only on the main thread, so each command starts and runs on another thread while the main thread waits. That thread is needed because `subprocess.Popen` took 1 to 23 ms to start the CLI on the macOS test host. An interrupt that landed there on the main thread left the container in 26 of 33 probe runs. Each command also runs in its own session, which a terminal Ctrl-C does not reach. In another probe, a SIGINT that reached the CLI during `docker create` left the container in 10 of 22 runs. On an idle daemon, with interrupts at 5 to 395 ms into a run, the old worker left the container in 1 of 80 runs after an interrupt to the host alone and in 7 of 80 after a Ctrl-C to the host's process group. This worker left none in either case.

Output passes only when all of these hold:

- The container exited 0 and was not killed for memory.
- The tar stream ends at its end marker. Extra bytes, such as a write the candidate forges onto the entrypoint's pipe, make it malformed.
- `/output` holds exactly one regular file, `signal.json`, no larger than the output limit.
- The file is UTF-8 JSON. It is one object without duplicate keys, every value is a finite number, and its keys equal the slice keys.

Each failure has its own reason: `timeout`, `memory`, `failed`, `missing_output`, `extra_output`, `oversized`, `malformed`, `non_finite`, or `wrong_index`. A refusal carries a fixed message from the module. It never carries the candidate's output or standard error.

Each container run carries a `RunRecord` of the image ID, the SHA-256 of the source, the adapter's `ReadRecord`, and the limits, in canonical JSON. Two runs of the same deterministic candidate over the same fixture give byte-identical records, and the read record replays the slice from the fixture bytes.

A `RunLog` keeps container runs in an existing directory as canonical JSON files numbered from `0.json`, read and written through the shared `read_log` and `append_log` of [D033](#d033-share-record-helpers-through-a-public-records-module). The caller appends what `run` returns. Each entry holds a new run ID, the `RunRecord`, the outcome, which is `signal` or the refusal reason, and for a signal the SHA-256 of the values' canonical JSON. A refused read started no container, so the log refuses it. Run IDs keep two identical runs as two entries, also when two writers append at once. Opening or appending replays every entry and raises `WorkerError` for non-canonical bytes, an unknown schema version or field, a gap, a stray file whose name does not start with a dot, a repeated run ID, an outcome that does not match its output, or a malformed image ID, limit, digest, or read record. A limit may be any JSON number, because Docker accepts a float memory limit. `append` checks its own entry with the same parser before writing, so it refuses a run that a reopen would reject, such as one whose limit is a bool, instead of writing it and locking the log. The [D039](#d039-register-verified-packages-in-surrealdb-and-keep-their-original-bytes-in-an-object-store) registry stores a run log's entries with their exact bytes through `registry.import_run_log`, and `registry.load_run_log` returns them.

The [D042](#d042-run-each-campaign-as-one-temporal-workflow-that-owns-its-logs) runner calls `run` through `execution.generated`, a body whose catalog, store, run context, run log, output directory, and limits come from the operator. A job's argument names only the source and the dataset. The body starts no container unless the revision that the attempt runs under sets the specification's `generated_code` section, which is false by default, as Phase 4 requires for this extension. A steer to a child revision can change it for later attempts. The body appends each container run to the run log, and the outcome detail names the logged run ID. A refused read started no container, so it fails the attempt without a log entry. A refusal for missing, extra, oversized, malformed, non-finite, or wrongly keyed output rejects the candidate, and the runner accepts that rejection as the job's result. A refused read, a timeout, a memory kill, or a nonzero exit fails the attempt, and the runner retries it within `max_attempts`. Both outcome details name the reason and its fixed message. A valid signal's values are written to the output directory as canonical JSON named by the logged output digest, and the successful outcome names the digest and path. A Docker failure, a missing image, or a run log that cannot be written raises `WorkerError`, which the runner records as a failed attempt. A runner test runs a valid, a malformed, and a slow candidate in a real container and checks each attempt against its log entry. In another runner test, the catalog refuses the read, so each attempt fails and the run log stays empty. In another, the researcher steers the running campaign to a child revision that sets `generated_code`. The generated job before the steer fails, and the generated job after it runs in a container.

The [D049](#d049-expose-evidence-to-agents-through-typed-tools-bound-to-the-run-context) agent tools call `run` through `run_signal`, with the run log, signal directory, and limits that the operator binds. A model names only the source and the dataset. D049 records the tool's gate, outcome, and replay.

[worker/Dockerfile](../worker/Dockerfile) builds the image from `python:3.12-slim` pinned by digest `sha256:cb33f7f771ecec2ef1225cf39b159591a040391bd73e4c01f00fdfc4c47611d2`, which holds Python 3.12.15. One layer adds the packages in [worker/requirements.txt](../worker/requirements.txt), which pip installs with `--require-hashes --no-deps --only-binary=:all:`. They are numpy 2.5.3 for array arithmetic and pandas 3.0.6 for time-indexed series, with python-dateutil 2.9.0.post0 and six 1.17.0, which pandas needs. The entrypoint sets `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS=1`. The container gets one CPU, and without the cap OpenBLAS starts one thread per host core. On this Docker Desktop VM, an import with a matrix product measured 14 threads and 98 MB peak resident memory (VmHWM) without the cap, and 1 thread and 72 MB with it. Both gave the same product.

`make worker-image` builds the image as `quant-research-worker:2` with `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH=0`, layer timestamps rewritten to it, and no provenance attestation. pip then writes hash-checked `.pyc` files. Two no-cache builds of a probe image with these options gave the same image ID, and two without them did not. That target is the only step that fetches from the network, and it fetches only the pinned base and the hashed wheels. Tests and `make verify` never pull.

To change the packages, edit [worker/requirements.in](../worker/requirements.in) and rerun the `uv pip compile` command at the top of the lock with a later `--exclude-newer` date. That date fixes which releases the compile can choose, so the same command gives the same lock. Then raise the tag number in `worker.IMAGE` and the Makefile, run `make worker-image`, and run the full suite. A new tag for each Dockerfile or lock change makes a machine that holds only the old image fail the container tests instead of running them against it.

The container tests skip only when no `docker` executable is on `PATH`. When Docker is installed but cannot supply the image, because the daemon is stopped or unreachable or the image is not built, every container test errors in setup with Docker's message, so `make verify` fails on that machine. A skip would hide the isolation checks, because the verify output prints how many tests skipped and not why.

Tests run each check in a real container. They attempt DNS, TCP, and UDP. They scan every readable file and every process environment for a credential planted in the host environment and a host file, and a control marker in the slice shows that the scan works. They also attempt writes outside scratch, run a fork loop, an allocation loop, and a sleep loop, raise a host-side exception mid-run, interrupt the host while a docker shim holds `docker create` or `docker rm` in flight or while the create CLI starts, and submit each kind of refused output. They import the locked packages and check their versions and the single BLAS thread.

These limits are deliberate:

- The image holds the standard library, the base image's pip, and the four locked packages. A candidate that imports anything else fails.
- The manual hosted workflow does not run `make worker-image`. On a runner with Docker, its container tests now error instead of skipping. Building the image on the runner is untested. [D074](#d074-run-the-full-suite-only-inside-make-merge) later deleted that workflow.
- The full image was built once, so its wheels were fetched once, and no second build compared its image ID. Only the arm64 image was built. The lock also lists the x86_64 wheel hashes.
- The tests ran on Docker Desktop 29.2.1 for macOS. Linux hosts and rootless Docker are untested, and gVisor and Kata are not used. The image build ran only on that Docker Desktop's containerd image store, where `rewrite-timestamp` requires `unpack=false`.
- Containers share the Docker VM's kernel. A kernel escape is outside this boundary.
- `/proc/self/mountinfo` shows the host path of the job directory. The path names a temporary directory and holds no secret.
- No host code runs after the host process receives SIGKILL, so a run killed that way leaves its labelled container for removal by hand. That container still has no network and keeps its limits.
- A second host exception ends the wait for the current docker command, so a second Ctrl-C can still leave a container. Against a hung daemon each command needs two, so a run stuck at create takes four Ctrl-Cs, and the CLIs it abandons keep running after the host exits.
- An interrupt in the microseconds after the thread starts, before the thread starts the CLI, cancels that command. For `docker rm` that leaves the container. This follows from the code and was not measured.
- The memory limit also counts tmpfs pages, so scratch and output space reduce it.
- A run log is a local directory. Nothing imports it into the registry automatically. A run stays only in that directory until the operator calls `registry.import_run_log`.

## D048. Review restricted confirmation reports through audited grants

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the restricted report that final confirmation issues in Phase 4 of the [master plan](master-plan.md), and for the review path that [D025](#d025-carry-restrictions-with-material-and-authority-with-the-run) leaves open. [quant_research.confirmation_review](../src/quant_research/confirmation_review.py) keeps restricted confirmation reports in a store of their own. Packages, source generations, and the review ledger never read it.

The store holds report files and one append-only log. `deposit_report` writes a report once under the ID of its confirmation exposure and binds it to a campaign that the caller declares. It writes the file only after the deposit event passes every check, so an invalid deposit writes nothing. A crash between the file and its event, or a deposit that loses its log position to a later-dated event and is then refused, leaves a file that no event names. Nothing can open it, and a valid repeat deposit completes it. That ID is already in the lineage history and is not a digest of the outcomes, so an export that names a report reveals nothing about its results. The deposit event holds the SHA-256 of the report file, and every open checks the file against it.

Grants, revocations, opens, and refusals are events in the same log, so grant state is a fold over it. A grant names one reviewer, one report or one campaign, one purpose (`human_review` or `audit`), and an exclusive expiry. A campaign grant also covers reports deposited after it. `open_report` returns the stored report only when an unrevoked, unexpired grant matches the reviewer, the report, and the purpose. Otherwise it records a refusal with its reasons and raises `OpenRefused`. An unknown report is refused as `no_grant`, so a reviewer who holds no grant covering a report learns nothing from a refusal about whether it exists. Callers supply every time, and a time earlier than the newest event raises without a record. Repeating a deposit, an identical active grant, or a revocation records nothing.

Each log entry is a canonical JSON file, numbered from zero, that holds the SHA-256 of the entry before it. Every read checks the numbering, the canonical bytes, and the chain, then refolds the log. The fold decides each recorded open and refusal again with the rule that `open_report` uses. An edit, removal, or reordering of an entry therefore fails unless every entry after it is rewritten too. A rechained open that no grant allowed fails either way. A writer claims the next number by creating its file exclusively, as the [capability exposure ledger](#d011-seal-capability-evaluation-answers-and-retire-disclosed-tasks) does. A writer that loses rereads the log and decides again, so a revocation that lands first refuses the open that raced it.

An open reveals confirmation outcomes, so each open yields an ordinary `ExposureEvent` of kind `result`. Its ID is the open event's ID, its actor is the reviewer, and its interval is the report's period. Its material digest is the frozen shortlist identity, because `save_package` requires every exposure's material to be one of the package's artifacts, and the shortlist manifest can be one. The caller appends the exposure to the lineage history, and `read_audit` rebuilds every review exposure from the log.

The report does not flow back:

- `save_package` refuses it as the record, as artifact content, and as the confirmation field, per [D021](#d021-gate-final-confirmation-on-exposure-history-and-frozen-shortlists). A report placed in any record field becomes plain data that holds its outcomes, so the report's own text representation, which omits them, no longer applies. Its `PackageError` therefore names the field and never quotes its value, and the shared [record parsers](../src/quant_research/records.py) take the field's name for their errors. They also keep a rejected value out of the exception chain, because Python's own UUID and timestamp errors can quote the text they fail to parse. A report in place of a whole trial, specification, or artifact raises `TypeError` or `AttributeError`, which quotes nothing. Before this change, a report in a UUID, timestamp, digest, status, kind, coverage, or artifact name field had its outcomes quoted in the error.
- `register_source` refuses it as source bytes and as a declaration field. The module returns no report bytes.
- The provider [call log](#d041-check-outbound-provider-content-against-the-run-and-a-versioned-provider-policy) refuses it in any field of a dispatch, call record, or refused charge, including the model record. The append writes nothing, and its `ProviderError` never quotes the value. Before this change the log stored the report's outcomes and then refused to reopen.
- The [review ledger](#d019-admit-lessons-only-through-recorded-review-events) now requires every text field of a proposed record, review, and supersession to be nonblank text. Before this change it admitted a lesson whose statement was the report object.
- No run context can clear confirmation material, as D025 decides. An open returns no context and changes no clearance, so a reviewer's own research contexts read exactly what they read before.
- Retrieval reads only published source generations through a pinned corpus, which the report cannot enter, and nothing reads this store.

These limits are deliberate:

- Reviewers, grantors, and depositors are declared principals. No identity provider authenticates them, and anyone who can call `grant_review` can grant.
- Caller-supplied times decide expiry, so expiry binds honest callers.
- The chain cannot vouch for the newest entry. A plain read misses the removal or rewrite of the newest entries, and of any entry whose later entries are rewritten with it. `read_audit` detects both against a head digest kept outside the store, and the review exposures in the lineage history are a second witness. Without such an external identity, a coordinated rewrite of the store goes undetected.
- Anyone who can read the store's files can read the reports. Grants bind callers of this module, not the file system.
- The deposit declares the campaign binding, and nothing checks it against a campaign revision.

## D049. Expose evidence to agents through typed tools bound to the run context

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the [Phase 4](master-plan.md#phase-4-complete-a-bounded-autonomous-campaign) requirement that agents reach evidence through a small set of typed tools whose authority comes from the trusted run context. [quant_research.agent_tools](../src/quant_research/agent_tools.py) defines the tools.

A `Toolset` binds one `RunContext`, the source store, the dataset store, the catalog, and the review log when the operator creates it. A run that enables generated code also binds a `Generated` of a run log, a signal directory, and container limits ([D047](#d047-run-generated-signal-code-in-a-locked-down-container)). A model reaches it only through `call`, which takes the tool name and the argument text the model sent. No tool takes a cutoff, clearance, corpus, provider, memory pin, or context argument, so a model cannot name a later cutoff, another corpus, or a later memory. Narrowing a context stays an operator operation.

| Tool | Arguments | Backing call | Result |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `search_corpus` | `mode`, `text` | `retrieve` over the context's pinned corpus ([D040](#d040-retrieve-exactly-over-a-pinned-eligible-corpus)) | The retrieval |
| `read_dataset` | `dataset` | `read` with the bound catalog ([D045](#d045-read-approved-datasets-through-a-timing-and-access-adapter)) | The supplied rows and their read record |
| `lookup_location` | `ref` | `published_locations`, limited to extractions of the pinned corpus ([D043](#d043-locate-paper-elements-in-published-extractions)) | The located element |
| `read_lessons` | none | `Ledger.admissible_lessons` at the context's memory pin ([D019](#d019-admit-lessons-only-through-recorded-review-events)) | The admissible lessons |
| `run_signal` | `source`, `dataset` | `worker.run` with the bound catalog, dataset store, and limits ([D047](#d047-run-generated-signal-code-in-a-locked-down-container)) | The container run's run log entry |

`read_lessons` finds the prefix of the bound review log whose head is the context's memory pin ([D025](#d025-carry-restrictions-with-material-and-authority-with-the-run)). It returns the lessons eligible in that prefix, honouring the [D046](#d046-suspend-records-whose-support-an-invalidation-removes) suspensions recorded by then, whose derived restrictions ([D019](#d019-admit-lessons-only-through-recorded-review-events)) the run may read as of its cutoff. Each lesson carries its record ID and every record field, in proposal order. A restricted lesson, a later one, one of unknown restrictions, one admitted after the pin, and one suspended before it are absent without a reason, as search omits units, so an empty result does not show that lessons exist. The pin selects governance state and declarations supply availability, so the review event times that `Ledger.as_of` takes still never meet the cutoff, as D045 requires. A bound log with no prefix at the pin is the operator's error and raises `ReviewError`.

Location lookup resolves a ref only in a locator generation whose extraction is also the extraction of a readable generation in the pinned corpus, as `corpus_members` ([D040](#d040-retrieve-exactly-over-a-pinned-eligible-corpus)) lists them. A ref into a source outside the corpus is therefore as unreachable as a source after the cutoff, and a location's offsets index the same text as the corpus chunks.

`run_signal` is the tool side of the generated-code gate. A toolset without a `Generated` refuses it as `generated_code_off` before any read, as the campaign runner refuses an attempt whose revision leaves `generated_code` off. Otherwise `worker.run` reads the slice first, so a refused read starts no container, appends no log entry, and returns the adapter's reason. The tool appends each container run to the bound run log and writes a valid signal's values to the signal directory as canonical JSON named by the logged output digest. Its outcome is the `LoggedRun`, with the run ID, the outcome, the output digest, and the run record. The outcome is `signal` or the worker's reason, whose fixed message comes from the worker. The result holds no signal values, and nothing the candidate prints reaches it. The tool description states D047's slice and output rules, the packages, and the absence of network.

Each tool is exposed as a name, a description, and a JSON Schema object for its arguments. Every property is a required string, `mode` adds an enum, and `additionalProperties` is false. `read_lessons` has no properties, so any key is refused. The schemas use no keyword beyond type, properties, required, additionalProperties, enum, and description, and the parser enforces every other rule. The specifications are a module constant, so no schema depends on a context.

Arguments arrive as the model's JSON text, and the module parses them before any store, catalog, or file access. The text must be one JSON object without a repeated key, with exactly the schema's properties, each a string that encodes as UTF-8. Anything else is refused as `invalid_arguments`, so an added `cutoff` or `generation` key refuses the call instead of being ignored. A query text that retrieval rejects and a ref that does not parse are also `invalid_arguments`.

A refusal is a reason code with a fixed message from one table. Messages quote no argument, label, dataset or source name, time, path, or stored text. The reasons are:

- `unknown_tool` and `invalid_arguments` for a malformed call.
- `generated_code_off` for `run_signal` in a run that does not enable generated code.
- `unknown_ref` for any ref that does not name a location the run may read in its pinned corpus. A ref into a restricted source, a later source, a source outside the corpus, and a ref to nothing get the same refusal, so a refusal never shows that restricted material exists.
- The dataset adapter's reasons and messages. An uncleared dataset is refused as `uncleared` and its labels are not named.

Search never refuses for clearance or cutoff. Ineligible units are absent, and BM25 statistics come from eligible units alone, so neither a refusal nor a changed ranking shows that hidden material matched.

A malformed call is the model's error and returns a refusal. An unpublished or damaged corpus, a malformed fixture, or a failed verification is the operator's error and raises the backing module's exception, which the runner must not relay to a model. A Docker failure, a missing worker image, or a run log that cannot be replayed or written raises `WorkerError`. The dataset adapter's parse error no longer quotes the fixture, because a read parses rows past the cutoff and clearance. Its exception chain keeps the parse detail for the operator, so a logged traceback can still quote parts of the fixture, such as a bad revision or dataset name. A rejected timestamp or text that cannot encode as UTF-8 leaves no fixture text in the chain, because the shared record helpers raise after their handlers.

Each call returns a `Call`. Its canonical JSON record has schema version 1 and holds the tool name and argument text as sent, the context identity, and one outcome:

- A refusal reason.
- The retrieval record of D040, with the supplied units and their text.
- The read record of D045, with the vintage, catalog digest, cutoff, and supplied key and revision pairs.
- The location's ref, source occurrence, locator generation digest, kind, span, and text.
- The memory pin and the returned lessons, each with its record ID.
- The run log entry of a container run, with its run ID, outcome, output digest, and run record.

`Toolset.replay` takes record bytes and raises `ToolError` when they are malformed or name another context identity. Otherwise it repeats the call and raises unless the new record's bytes equal the old. A record therefore replays byte-identically under the same context, stores, catalog, and review log. A lessons record still replays after the log grows, because the pin selects the same prefix. A changed record, catalog, fixture, or store fails replay. A record cannot widen access, because replay repeats only the recorded call under the bound context.

Replay does not run a recorded container run again, because a second run would get a new run ID. It repeats the read, and the bound run log must hold the recorded entry. That entry's run record must name the digest of the recorded source, the repeated read record, and the bound limits. Replay of a run therefore starts no container and needs no Docker. A run log without the entry, a changed catalog or fixture, other limits, another source, or a toolset without generated code fails replay. A refused read replays like any refusal.

These limits are deliberate:

- Nothing calls the tools. The campaign runner, a provider adapter, and the disclosure history wire them in their own PRs.
- Replay of a container run checks the run log and does not repeat the run, so it does not show that the candidate still produces its signal. It shows that the log holds a run of those inputs, not that this call wrote it, and it does not check that the image still exists.
- `call` blocks while a container runs. A caller with an event loop runs it in a thread, as `execution.generated` runs the worker and the run log through `asyncio.to_thread`.
- A record holds what each backing call returned, not the text a model received. [D059](#d059-render-each-tool-call-as-gateway-material-under-the-restrictions-of-what-it-read) renders each call as gateway material.
- The corpus does not pin dataset vintages. The store keeps one fixture per dataset, so replay raises after it changes, as D045 states.
- Location lookup reopens every published generation and locator generation on each call, like the exact retrieval baseline.
- No tool lists locations. A model looks up a ref it already holds, such as one from a reproduction specification.
- A tool name or argument text with an unpaired surrogate cannot be recorded, so `to_bytes` and `replay` raise `PackageError`. An adapter that encodes a parsed object as argument text must keep JSON escapes, as `json.dumps` does by default.
- A pin fixes memory. A suspension, retraction, or declaration appended after it reaches only a context issued a later pin, so an operator pins the log that the registry's `load_ledger` returns, after the [D046](#d046-suspend-records-whose-support-an-invalidation-removes) catch-up. For sends, [D059](#d059-render-each-tool-call-as-gateway-material-under-the-restrictions-of-what-it-read) applies declarations appended after the pin.
- Restrictions derive from citations. Knowledge an author used without citing it escapes them, and admission records the reviewer's judgment that the citations are complete.
- No path declares restrictions from the source, dataset, or package stores yet, so tests declare them. Existing lessons stay unreadable to agents until their evidence is declared.
- A lessons record holds no restrictions. [D059](#d059-render-each-tool-call-as-gateway-material-under-the-restrictions-of-what-it-read) gives each rendered call the restrictions of what it read.
- No tool searches or ranks lessons. `read_lessons` returns every admissible lesson.
- The labels, approvals, and grants in tests are test policies. Phase 0 grants replace them.

## D050. Run the Phase 0 pilot locally on public US macro data

Selected on 22 October 2026 for [Phase 0](master-plan.md#phase-0-define-the-reference-campaign). The [Phase 0 pilot brief](phase0-pilot.md) holds the specification.

The firm's backtester, signal evaluator, data services, model proxy, and research database are not available yet. The master plan says that missing framework access must result in "a narrower pilot or a named prerequisite". The pilot therefore runs locally on public data and local services, and each firm resource is a named prerequisite of the port.

The researcher holds every Phase 0 role and selected these choices:

- The real-time Sahm rule, computed from ALFRED vintages of `UNRATE`, is the Phase 1 known-answer case. FRED publishes the same series, and the annual seasonal revisions test revision timing.
- Estrella and Mishkin (1998) is the Phase 3 paper. It shares the recession-prediction domain and uses public rate series.
- Ken French monthly market and risk-free returns are the traded returns, and vectorbt 1.2.0 is the local backtester.
- Confirmation is prospective only, because the researcher and the models already know the sample's history.

Local acceptance is a scoped Phase 1. It keeps every Phase 1 exit test and runs them on real public data and a real backtester, not on synthetic fixtures. It does not accept the firm frameworks. The port is a separate, later acceptance. The firm adapters first rerun the same frozen public inputs and must agree with the local results, and only then does the pilot move to firm data.

The repository adds an interface only where the pilot itself substitutes one implementation for another. The brief lists the responsibilities that change at the port, so the port has a checklist. No placeholder adapter is written for a firm system, and each firm interface is designed from that system's inspected calls.

These limits are deliberate:

- Review is not independent, because one person holds every role.
- Capability-evaluation tasks that the researcher writes are development cases, because their author also develops and steers the configurations they evaluate.
- vectorbt 1.2.0 was released on 19 October 2026 and is unproven on this project's Python 3.12. Its license is Apache 2.0 with the Commons Clause, which forbids selling a service whose value derives mainly from vectorbt.
- ALFRED's real-time dates show when ALFRED published a value, not when a firm received it.
- None of the pilot's data, reconciliation, or backtests exist yet, and Phase 0 is not complete until the probes in the [to dos](todos.md) pass.

## D051. Own the research loop and call subscriptions as tool-disabled completions

Selected on 22 October 2026 for the local pilot's model calls. The researcher wants local inference to run on their own Claude Code and Codex subscriptions. The firm port will call a firm model proxy whose transport is not yet known.
